When punishment is combined with mechanisms for reputation-building (e.g., non-anonymity, indirect reciprocity), cooperation is further enhanced and the use of punishment becomes more targeted and less frequent, reducing its destructive impact. The author argues that these findings are directly relevant for designing fisheries management institutions: enabling peer monitoring, punishment, and communication can foster cooperation and improve collective outcomes. However, the paper also cautions that externally imposed sanctions may undermine intrinsic motivation to cooperate, and that institutional legitimacy and stakeholder involvement are crucial.
  prediction_guidance: This paper should inform predictions that enabling punishment in public goods game-like environments (including fisheries) generally increases efficiency and group payoff, especially when combined with mechanisms for reputation and communication. The evidence is based on a synthesis of experimental studies, not new data, but the translation to fisheries is conceptually strong. The paper highlights that the effect of punishment is most positive when it is peer-driven, combined with transparency and communication, and not imposed externally. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment, reputation, and communication mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control games without these features.

- source: Milinski, M. (2016). Reputation, a universal currency for human social interactions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that punishment in public goods games can maintain high levels of cooperation, but often at a net cost due to the expenses of punishing and being punished. Reputation mechanisms, such as indirect reciprocity, can also sustain cooperation and do so more efficiently by avoiding these costs. When both punishment and reputation mechanisms are available, punishment is used less frequently and more selectively, and overall group efficiency is increased. The review also notes that reward mechanisms are generally less effective than punishment in sustaining cooperation. The findings are based on a synthesis of multiple experimental studies, with particular emphasis on the interaction between punishment and reputation in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games generally increases cooperation and can improve group efficiency, especially when combined with reputation mechanisms. However, punishment alone may not always increase efficiency due to its costs. The review provides indirect evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the presence of reputation systems and the ability to observe or communicate about others' actions. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and is maximized when reputation mechanisms are also present. The review does not provide new quantitative estimates but synthesizes qualitative and comparative findings from the literature.

- source: Peysakhovich, A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Habits of Virtue: Creating Norms of Cooperation and Defection in the Laboratory. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Exposure to environments that support cooperation (via repeated PDs with high continuation probability and favorable payoffs) leads to higher prosociality in subsequent one-shot games, including the public goods game, trust game, dictator game, and ultimatum game. In a second experiment, exposure to cooperative environments increases third-party punishment of selfishness and reduces antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment. These effects are strongest among subjects who rely on intuitive (heuristic) decision-making. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is not assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional environments can shape prosocial and punishment behaviors in public-goods-game-like settings, and that these behaviors spill over into new contexts. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency. The design details (e.g., group size, punishment cost/tech, MPCR) are reported and can inform mapping to other studies, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Chen, X. P., Pillutla, M. M., & Yao, X. (2009). Unintended Consequences of Cooperation Inducing and Maintaining Mechanisms in Public Goods Dilemmas: Sanctions and Moral Appeals. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Sanctions (punishment or reward) in public goods games increase cooperation while present, but when removed, cooperation drops sharply—sometimes to levels below those in groups that never had sanctions. Moral appeals also increase cooperation, but this effect is more persistent after the appeal is removed. The drop in cooperation after removing sanctions is mediated by reduced trust in others' cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are based on contribution rates, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (and reward) increases cooperation in the short term, but this effect is not sustained after removal, and may even backfire. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes for games with punishment enabled versus disabled. The findings are most useful for predicting contribution rates and for understanding the psychological mechanisms (trust) that may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. For efficiency prediction, the results suggest caution: increased cooperation under punishment may not translate to sustained efficiency gains, especially if punishment is later removed.

- source: Kiyonari, T., & Barclay, P. (2008). Cooperation in social dilemmas: Free riding may be thwarted by second-order reward rather than by punishment. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that, in one-shot public goods games, participants are much more likely to support cooperation through reward (both first- and second-order) than through punishment. First-order punishment of defectors occurs, but second-order punishment of nonpunishers is rare or absent, and punishers are not socially endorsed. In contrast, both first- and second-order rewards are common, and rewarders are socially endorsed. The presence of both punishment and reward options does not increase support for punishment. The paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in one-shot, four-player public goods games with binary contribution and surprise sanctioning opportunities, punishment is not socially supported at the second order, and reward is more likely to be used and socially endorsed. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar game designs, punishment may not be self-sustaining and may not improve efficiency unless supported by other mechanisms. The findings are most relevant for predicting the likelihood of punishment and reward behaviors, not their direct impact on efficiency.

- source: Pfattheicher, S., Keller, J., & Knezevic, G. (2017). Sadism, the Intuitive System, and Antisocial Punishment in the Public Goods Game. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The studies show that activating the intuitive system increases antisocial punishment among individuals with high sadism, while inhibiting intuition reduces it. This effect is specific to antisocial punishment and not to punishment of defectors or to contribution behavior. The presence of punishment in the game enables antisocial punishment, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological moderators of antisocial punishment but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and cognitive moderators of antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, but it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is only indirectly informative: it confirms that antisocial punishment can occur in standard PGGs with punishment, and that its frequency is affected by individual differences and cognitive processing mode. However, it does not quantify the impact of punishment on efficiency or provide control vs. treatment efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper can help confirm the mapping of design features, but not the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: Gächter, S., Herrmann, B., & Thöni, C. (2010). Culture and cooperation. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a standard public goods game, the opportunity to punish increases average contributions in some cultures but not in others. Cultural background explains a much larger share of the variance in contributions when punishment is available than when it is not. Punishment patterns themselves (including antisocial punishment) differ strongly across cultures and are linked to differences in cooperation. The presence of punishment reduces within-group variance in contributions but has mixed effects on between-group variance. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the punishment condition in some cultures suggest likely higher efficiency in those cases, while in other cultures the effect is negligible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) is highly culture-dependent, even in otherwise identical public goods game designs. For prediction tasks, this means that game design dimensions alone (such as player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are not sufficient to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency without accounting for cultural context. The paper's results suggest that, in some environments, punishment can substantially increase cooperation (and likely efficiency), while in others it has little or no effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its quantitative value for efficiency prediction is limited to inferring that higher contributions generally imply higher efficiency.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2011). Punish in public. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Privately-implemented weak punishment reduces cooperation compared to a baseline with no punishment, while publicly-implemented punishment (where all group members are informed of punishment events) increases and sustains cooperation at higher levels than both baseline and private punishment. The effect is not due to shame, signaling, or differences in information about contributions, as all treatments provide full information about group contributions and maintain anonymity. The public punishment mechanism makes the cooperation norm more salient, leading to higher voluntary and conditional cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the higher contribution rates in the public punishment treatment suggest that public punishment can reverse the detrimental effects of weak punishment on group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the public observability of punishment (even when the punished individual's identity is anonymous) is a key moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation in public goods games. When predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, the design dimension of whether punishment is public or private is critical: private, weak punishment can reduce cooperation below baseline, while public punishment can increase it above baseline. Although the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the close mapping between contribution rates and group welfare in this linear PGG means that higher contributions in the public punishment treatment likely translate to higher efficiency. The findings are most applicable to settings with small groups, fixed partners, linear PGG structure, and exogenous, weak punishment. The results are less informative for settings with endogenous punishment, strong sanctions, or different information structures.

- source: Pleasant, A., & Barclay, P. (2018). Why Hate the Good Guy? Antisocial Punishment of High Cooperators Is Greater When People Compete To Be Chosen. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that when participants in a public goods game with punishment are competing to be chosen for a subsequent trust game, antisocial punishment (punishing high contributors) is much higher than in a standard PGG with punishment. This effect is present from the first round and persists across rounds. In the control condition, contributions increase over rounds (as is typical when punishment is available), but in the markets condition, contributions do not increase, suggesting that antisocial punishment suppresses the escalation of cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral pattern implies that the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment may be undermined or reversed in contexts with high antisocial punishment driven by partner competition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the context of partner competition (biological markets) can substantially increase antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, which in turn suppresses the usual increase in cooperation seen with punishment. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the findings imply that in environments where partner choice or competitive altruism is salient, enabling punishment may not improve and may even harm group efficiency, as antisocial punishment undermines cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of partner competition or similar social comparison incentives is a critical moderator: in such settings, the efficiency benefit of punishment is likely reduced or reversed, even if control efficiency is known. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Carpenter, J. P., & Matthews, P. H. (2012). NORM ENFORCEMENT: ANGER, INDIGNATION, OR RECIPROCITY?. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both second-party and third-party punishment increase contributions in a one-shot public goods game, with the effect size increasing as opportunities for third-party punishment and reciprocity increase. The order of mean contributions is: no punishment < second-party punishment < one-way third-party punishment < simultaneous two-way third-party punishment < sequential two-way third-party punishment. The study finds that third-party punishment is motivated by both indignation and group reciprocity, and that the presence of third-party punishment increases the expected cost of norm violation, leading to higher contributions. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (especially third-party punishment with reciprocity) increases contributions in a one-shot public goods game, relative to a no-punishment baseline. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on contribution rates, and suggest that the structure of punishment (second-party vs. third-party, reciprocity opportunities) is an important moderator. For efficiency prediction, the results imply that efficiency is likely to increase with punishment, but the magnitude cannot be directly inferred from the reported data.

- source: Grossman, G., & Baldassarri, D. (2012). The Impact of Elections on Cooperation: Evidence from a Lab-in-the-Field Experiment in Uganda. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The introduction of a centralized sanctioning authority (monitor) in a PGG increases contributions to the public good compared to a baseline with no punishment. When the monitor is elected rather than randomly assigned, contributions increase further, suggesting a legitimacy effect. Both types of monitors punish defectors at a personal cost, but the actual punishment strategies are similar between elected and random monitors. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The findings are robust to controls for monitor characteristics and are supported by observational data linking legitimacy to cooperation in real-world farmer groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing centralized punishment in a PGG increases contributions, and that the legitimacy of the punisher (via election) further increases cooperation. However, because the main outcome is contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the experimental manipulation is clear, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff reporting limits the precision of predictions for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gächter, S., & Herrmann, B. (2009). Reciprocity, culture and human cooperation: previous insights and a new cross-cultural experiment. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in one-shot public goods games, the introduction of punishment opportunities increases contributions in Swiss participant pools but not in Russian pools. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is much more common in Russia, leading to lower earnings and less effective cooperation. The paper reports that 80% of Russian participants earned less than the selfish prediction, compared to 33% in Switzerland. However, the study does not directly report group efficiency (payoff relative to the cooperative maximum) or compare average payoffs between punishment and no-punishment conditions. The findings highlight that the effect of punishment on cooperation and earnings is strongly moderated by cultural context, with punishment sometimes failing to improve, or even reducing, group welfare due to antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and earnings in public goods games is highly context-dependent, especially on cultural background. While the design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are well specified, the key moderator in this study is culture, which is not a standard game design variable. The paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency (payoff relative to maximum) from control efficiency and design dimensions, but it does show that punishment can increase, decrease, or have no effect on group earnings depending on the prevalence of antisocial punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of antisocial punishment (which may be proxied by cultural or social variables) is a critical moderator, and that design dimensions alone may be insufficient to predict efficiency effects of punishment without accounting for such context.

- source: Espín, A. M., Brañas-Garza, P., Herrmann, B., & Gamella, J. F. (2012). Patient and impatient punishers of free-riders. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that punishment in a one-shot public goods game is implemented by two distinct types of individuals: patient cooperators (who punish free-riders, likely for moralistic/future-oriented reasons) and impatient free-riders (who punish other free-riders, likely for competitive/spiteful reasons). The likelihood of punishing is positively associated with cooperation among patient individuals and with free-riding among impatient individuals. The paper does not analyze or report the effect of punishment on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. It does, however, offer insight into the psychological heterogeneity of punishers, which may be relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind punishment behavior. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable data on efficiency outcomes or how game design dimensions affect efficiency with or without punishment. Its main value is in highlighting that the motivations for punishment can differ (moralistic vs. competitive) depending on individual patience and contribution behavior, but this is not tied to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Baldassarri, D., & Grossman, G. (2011). Centralized sanctioning and legitimate authority promote cooperation in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Centralized sanctioning, where a single monitor can punish, increases contributions to the public good compared to a baseline with no punishment. When the monitor is elected (legitimate authority), contributions increase even more than when the monitor is randomly assigned. The effect is attributed to the legitimacy of the authority, not to differences in the monitors' intrinsic characteristics or sanctioning behavior. The study is conducted in the field with Ugandan farmer cooperatives, using a repeated PGG with 8-12 players per group. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that centralized punishment (by a single monitor) increases cooperation (contributions) in public goods games, and that legitimacy of the authority (election vs. random assignment) further enhances this effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting the effect of centralized punishment and authority legitimacy on contribution rates, and suggest that similar effects may be expected for efficiency if contributions translate directly to group payoff in the game design. The study is highly relevant for understanding the impact of centralized punishment and authority legitimacy, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2010). Cooperative behavior cascades in human social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in both standard and punishment-enabled public goods games, individuals' contributions are causally influenced by the contributions of others in their group, and this influence spreads to others in subsequent rounds and groups, up to three degrees of separation. The presence of punishment increases contributions (as established in the original Fehr & Gachter 2002 study), but in this analysis, punishment does not fundamentally change the pattern or extent of behavioral cascades. Punishment can trigger cooperative cascades, but punishment behavior itself does not spread. The main outcome is the spread and persistence of cooperative behavior, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the spread of cooperative behavior in public goods games with and without punishment, but it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does confirm that punishment increases contributions (as in the original Fehr & Gachter 2002), and that the presence of punishment does not fundamentally alter the network dynamics of cooperation spread. For efficiency prediction, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify payoff or efficiency effects.

- source: Wu, J. J., Li, C., Zhang, B. Y., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2014). The role of institutional incentives and the exemplar in promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Institutional punishment increases average group contributions above control and reward-only treatments, and the combination of reward and punishment (IRP) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The effect is robust across three incentive schemes (fixed, increasing, decreasing). The study finds that individuals react more strongly to punishment than to reward, especially among high contributors. The presence of 'cooperating-individuals' (exemplars) in a group is associated with higher average contributions, as conformists tend to follow their lead. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff, but the design and behavioral findings are highly relevant for understanding how institutional punishment affects cooperation in repeated PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional punishment (and especially combined reward and punishment) increases average group contributions in repeated PGGs with four players and 50 rounds. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the contribution results suggest that punishment is likely to improve efficiency relative to control, especially when combined with reward. The experimental design and manipulation of incentive schemes are informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG settings, but direct quantitative prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone due to the lack of payoff/efficiency reporting.

- source: Weng, Q., & Carlsson, F. (2015). Cooperation in teams: The role of identity, punishment, and endowment distribution. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) strong identity (via human knot) increases contribution rates in heterogeneous teams without punishment, but not in homogeneous teams; (2) strong identity (via online chat) increases contribution rates in homogeneous teams; (3) punishment increases contribution rates when punishment is effective (1:3), even under strong identity, but not when punishment is less effective (1:2) and identity is strong; (4) strong identity does not further enhance contributions or punishment when punishment is available; (5) punishment of deviations from the team average does not depend on the target's endowment. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (as measured by contribution rate) depends on the effectiveness of punishment and the strength of identity-building. When punishment is effective (punishment impact per unit cost is high), it increases contributions even under strong identity. When punishment is less effective and identity is strong, punishment does not increase contributions. However, the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, for similar public good games, enabling effective punishment is likely to increase efficiency (by increasing contributions), but the effect may be moderated by identity-building and punishment effectiveness. The absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). Undermining trust and cooperation: The paradox of sanctioning systems in social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that sanctioning systems in public goods games can undermine trust in others' intrinsic motivation to cooperate. When the sanctioning system is removed, both trust and cooperation decrease, especially among participants with high initial trust. The negative effect on cooperation is mediated by the reduction in trust. The effect is stronger for large sanctions and when initial trust is high. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment system can undermine trust and, after its removal, reduce cooperation, especially when initial trust is high. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on baseline trust levels and that the removal of punishment can have negative aftereffects on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and potential moderators (such as initial trust and punishment size), rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Krupp, D. B., Debruine, L. A., & Barclay, P. (2008). A cue of kinship promotes cooperation for the public good. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that subtle cues of kinship (facial self-resemblance) increase cooperation in a public goods game with punishment. Participants contributed more to the public good as the number of self-resembling (kin-cue) group members increased, but did not change their punishment behavior toward free riders based on kinship cues. The experiment used a yoked control design to isolate the effect of kinship cues. The main outcomes are contribution and punishment levels; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured. The findings suggest that kinship cues can promote cooperation, but do not alter punitive responses to free riding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a standard public goods game with punishment, increasing perceived kinship among group members increases contributions but does not affect punishment rates. While efficiency is not directly measured, higher contributions generally imply higher efficiency, unless offset by excessive punishment costs. Since punishment rates did not increase, it is reasonable to infer that efficiency would likely increase with kinship cues. However, the paper does not manipulate or analyze any of the core game design dimensions (such as MPCR, punishment cost, or player count) except for the kinship cue. Thus, its direct value for predicting efficiency as a function of game design dimensions is limited, but it does suggest that social context (kinship cues) can moderate cooperation independently of punishment mechanics.

- source: Lubell, M., & Scholz, J. T. (2001). Cooperation, reciprocity, and the collective-action heuristic. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that both reciprocity and niceness in the environment increase cooperation rates, but their effects depend on whether subjects are initially predisposed to cooperate or defect. Past experience with reciprocity increases cooperation even when current reciprocity is absent, while past nonreciprocity does not reduce cooperation when reciprocity is present. The introduction of punishment (penalties) increases cooperation among defectors and in nonreciprocal environments, but can reduce cooperation among initial cooperators in reciprocal environments. The effect of punishment is thus asymmetric: it is most beneficial in environments lacking reciprocity, but may undermine cooperation where social capital is already high. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not direct efficiency or group payoff, but the authors discuss the implications for the evolution of cooperation and social capital.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends on the underlying level of reciprocity and the initial disposition of players. Punishment increases cooperation in environments where reciprocity is low (nonreciprocal), but can reduce cooperation among those already inclined to cooperate in high-reciprocity environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive in low-cooperation, low-reciprocity settings, but may be neutral or even negative in high-cooperation, high-reciprocity settings. However, since the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, these implications are inferred from cooperation rates. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are well specified, but the main outcome is behavioral (cooperation), not payoff-based.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Punishment, and Cooperation Across 18 Societies: A Meta-Analysis. *Perspectives on Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase cooperation in public goods games, with a moderate average effect (d=0.77, 95% CI [0.63, 0.91]), but with substantial cross-societal variation. The effect of punishment is significantly stronger in high-trust societies. Methodological moderators show that punishment is more effective in partner-matching protocols (fixed groups) and with more iterations (rounds), but not significantly affected by group size. Other societal variables (GDP, democracy, market competition, religious participation, norms of civic cooperation) have weaker or non-significant effects after controlling for trust. The outcome measured is behavioral cooperation/contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the standardized Fehr & Gächter public goods game, enabling peer punishment increases cooperation rates, especially in high-trust societies. However, the outcome is contribution/cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone. The findings suggest that, for lab PGGs with 3-7 players, 6-50 rounds, and standard costly peer punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MUs), punishment increases cooperation, with larger effects in partner-matching and longer games. The effect on efficiency is likely positive if higher cooperation translates to higher group payoffs, but this is not directly measured. The paper is highly informative for predicting behavioral cooperation under different game designs and societal contexts, but less so for direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Alexander, M., & Christia, F. (2011). Context Modularity of Human Altruism. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment (sanctions) increases contributions to the public good, but only in institutional contexts characterized by integration (i.e., integrated schools). In segregated institutional contexts, the availability of punishment does not increase contributions. Group diversity (ethnic heterogeneity) reduces contributions in segregated contexts but not in integrated ones. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency would be positive in integrated contexts and negligible in segregated contexts, assuming higher contributions translate to higher group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (proxied by contributions) is highly context-dependent: punishment increases contributions only when institutional integration is present. The game design dimensions most directly supported are player count (4), number of rounds (20), continuous contribution, no chat, and the presence/absence of punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must infer from contribution rates. The findings suggest that, in similar field settings, enabling punishment will only improve efficiency if the institutional context supports integration among participants.

- source: Fang, Y. H., Benko, T. P., Perc, M., Xu, H. Y., & Tan, Q. M. (2019). Synergistic third-party rewarding and punishment in the public goods game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment and reward, when applied together, strongly promote cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The synergy between punishment and reward is more effective than either alone, especially at lower cost to the third party. Lower thresholds for intervention (greater tolerance for defection) and higher intensity of intervention both increase cooperation. More frequent intervention (shorter time intervals) also promotes cooperation. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the combination of punishment and reward can robustly increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, and that the effect is sensitive to the threshold, intensity, and frequency of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its findings are most useful for understanding how design dimensions like intervention intensity, threshold, and frequency affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings, but this is not demonstrated here.

- source: Rockenbach, B., & Milinski, M. (2011). To qualify as a social partner, humans hide severe punishment, although their observed cooperativeness is decisive. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that in a public goods game with punishment, players strategically hide low contributions and severe punishment from an observer who may select future partners. Observers focus on observed contributions when making partner choices, largely ignoring punishment behavior. Punishment is used mainly to discipline low contributors, and its presence increases average contributions compared to the no-punishment game. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a main outcome, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment on efficiency directly. The main findings are about behavioral strategies for hiding and observing, and the determinants of partner selection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment increases average contributions in a public goods game, but does not directly report on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, it supports the idea that punishment can raise cooperation, but the effect on efficiency must be inferred indirectly. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are reported and can be mapped, but the main outcomes are behavioral rather than payoff-based. The presence of strategic hiding and observation may moderate the effect of punishment, but the paper does not quantify this in terms of efficiency. Thus, the paper is informative for understanding behavioral mechanisms, but less directly useful for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., Zettler, I., & Heydasch, T. (2012). Personality, Punishment and Public Goods: Strategic Shifts Towards Cooperation as a Matter of Dispositional Honesty-Humility. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The presence of punishment in a PGG increases individual contributions to the public good, replicating prior findings. This effect is moderated by the Honesty-Humility personality trait: individuals low in Honesty-Humility increase their contributions substantially when punishment is possible, while those high in Honesty-Humility contribute at a high level regardless of punishment. The study uses hypothetical (non-incentivized) PGGs, and the main outcome is contribution behavior, not actual efficiency or group payoff. A simulation based on observed contributions suggests that group efficiency (total group gain) increases with the number of high Honesty-Humility members, but this is not an experimental outcome. The findings are robust across two experiments, including one with a large, heterogeneous sample and a substantial time lag between personality and PGG measurement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment increases contributions in PGGs, especially among individuals low in Honesty-Humility. However, because the experiments are hypothetical and do not use real monetary incentives, and because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured as an experimental outcome, the findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates rather than efficiency per se. The simulation of group efficiency based on observed contributions provides indirect evidence that punishment could increase efficiency, but this is not a direct experimental result. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers informative indirect evidence that punishment increases contributions (and thus likely efficiency), but the lack of real incentives and direct efficiency measurement are important limitations.

- source: Baldassarri, D. (2015). Cooperative Networks: Altruism, Group Solidarity, Reciprocity, and Sanctioning in Ugandan Producer Organizations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both communication and the threat of sanctioning increase contributions in a repeated PGG compared to baseline. Communication (reciprocity) has a stronger effect than punishment. However, only cooperation in the communication condition predicts real-life cooperative behaviors in the farmer organizations. The threat of sanctioning increases contributions in the lab, but this does not translate to real-world cooperation among council members. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in small, cohesive groups with repeated interaction, communication (reciprocity) is a more effective and relevant mechanism for promoting cooperation than punishment. While punishment increases contributions in the lab PGG, it does not predict real-world cooperative behavior in this context. The absence of efficiency or payoff data means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it suggests that in similar settings, punishment may not translate into real-world cooperative gains. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that communication/reciprocity is a key moderator, and that punishment's effect may be context-dependent and less robust in small, high-trust groups.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Lun, J. (2015). Societal threat and cultural variation in the strength of social norms: An evolutionary basis. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the PGG with punishment, higher societal threat leads to higher rates of cooperation and a greater proportion of norm-enforcing punishers. Under low threat, cooperation and norm enforcement are weaker. The presence of punishment is necessary to achieve high cooperation under threat; spatial structure alone is insufficient. The model also shows that norm strength (adherence and enforcement) increases rapidly in response to increased threat and declines gradually when threat is removed. While payoffs are modeled, the main reported outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement in PGG-like environments, especially under higher threat. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment enabled/disabled, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The model parameters (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) are specified, and the model is robust to parameter changes, but the main outcomes are behavioral. The paper supports the qualitative prediction that enabling punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement, which is likely to increase efficiency, but does not provide direct efficiency data for prediction tasks.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Rong, Z. H. (2015). Mutual punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study uses agent-based simulations of a spatial public goods game on a square lattice, where each player participates in five overlapping groups. The key manipulation is the introduction of a symmetric, mutual punishment: whenever two neighbors have different strategies, both pay a punishment fine. The main result is that higher punishment fines lead to higher steady-state cooperation rates, and for sufficiently high fines, the population becomes fully cooperative. The mechanism is robust to changes in system size and noise. The paper provides a formula for the payoff difference between cooperators and defectors at the interface between clusters, showing that this difference increases with the punishment fine, favoring the spread of cooperation. However, the paper does not report group-level efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mutual punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially as the punishment fine increases. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not efficiency. The model is theoretical and does not include communication, reward, or other institutional features. The punishment is symmetric and always applied when strategies differ, which is a specific variant of punishment not always present in experimental or real-world settings.

- source: Van Vugt, M., & De Cremer, D. (1999). Leadership in social dilemmas: The effects of group identification on collective actions to provide public goods. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Instrumental (punishing) leaders increase contributions to the public good, especially when group identification is low. (2) Relational (encouraging) leaders only increase contributions when group identification is high. (3) In low group identification groups, relational leaders are ineffective and may even be counterproductive. (4) The legitimacy of the leader mediates the effect on contributions. (5) There is a general preference for democratic, elected, and internal leaders, especially when group identification is high. (6) No direct group payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, but the pattern of contribution changes is clear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment (via an instrumental leader) in increasing contributions depends on group identification. In low group identification settings, punishment is much more effective than encouragement. In high group identification settings, both punishment and encouragement can be effective. However, the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are partially specified and can inform dimension coding, but the main outcome is contribution, not efficiency.

- source: Lindström, B., Jangard, S., Selbing, I., & Olsson, A. (2018). The Role of a Common Is Moral Heuristic in the Stability and Change of Moral Norms. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across nine experiments, participants observing one-shot PGGs judged common behaviors (whether altruistic or selfish) as more moral and less deserving of punishment than rare behaviors. This effect was robust across lab and online samples, and held for both preexisting moral norms (cooperation/selfishness) and arbitrary behaviors. In one experiment, participants could spend real money to punish, and commonness reduced costly punishment of selfishness. The effect of commonness was explained by a formal model of social influence (Social Impact Theory). Agent-based simulations showed that the CIM heuristic can generate both stability and sudden change in group-level moral norms. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived commonness of behaviors in PGGs shapes moral and punishment judgments, and that common selfishness is punished less than rare selfishness. It also shows that costly punishment is reduced when selfishness is common. However, because the experiments are one-shot and do not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, the findings do not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The results are most useful for understanding psychological and behavioral responses to norm violation and punishment, rather than for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions in repeated or strategic PGGs.

- source: Stagnaro, M. N., Arechar, A. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). From good institutions to generous citizens: Top-down incentives to cooperate promote subsequent prosociality but not norm enforcement. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG (with 3 players, 10 rounds, and varying inspection probabilities) leads to a substantial increase in contribution rates compared to a no-punishment control. This institutional exposure also increases subsequent prosociality in a Dictator Game, but does not directly increase punishment behavior in a Third-Party Punishment Game. The effect on punishment is only indirect, mediated by increased prosociality. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG increases contribution rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly. The design dimensions (player count, number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the manipulation of punishment is clear. However, for the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper only provides indirect support via contribution rates. It does not allow direct estimation of efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that even minimal punishment is sufficient to substantially increase cooperation, and that further increases in punishment probability do not yield additional gains in contribution. The absence of direct efficiency data is a limitation for prediction.

- source: Lv, S. J., & Song, F. F. (2022). Particle swarm intelligence and the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game with punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of punishment on cooperation in a spatial public goods game depends on both the punishment intensity and the learning rule (PSO weighting coefficient). For low punishment intensity, low susceptibility to neighbors (low omega) promotes cooperation; for high punishment intensity, high omega is more beneficial. Intermediate omega values increase punishment input but can reduce cooperation. The enhancement factor (r) also moderates these effects. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are about cooperation rates and investment allocation, not direct welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and learning rules affect cooperation rates and investment allocation in spatial PGGs with continuous strategies. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment. For the prediction task, the main value is in understanding that the effect of punishment on cooperation is highly sensitive to punishment intensity and the learning rule, and that the allocation of resources between cooperation and punishment can affect the expansion of cooperative clusters. However, since efficiency is not reported, the paper's findings should be used only as indirect, mechanistic context rather than as direct quantitative evidence for efficiency prediction.

- source: Liu, J. Z., Meng, H. R., Wang, W., Li, T., & Yu, Y. (2018). Synergy punishment promotes cooperation in spatial public good game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment into a spatial public goods game increases the fraction of cooperators, and that further reducing the cost of punishment through a synergistic effect (i.e., when multiple punishers share the cost) leads to even higher cooperation rates. The effect of synergy is most pronounced when the punishment fine is low; at high fines, the additional benefit of synergy is minimal. The main outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The paper qualitatively links higher cooperation to higher group payoffs, but does not report efficiency or payoff data directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both punishment and reduced punishment cost (via synergy) increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting how design dimensions like punishment cost and punishment effectiveness affect cooperation rates, and by extension may suggest (but not demonstrate) positive effects on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect support for the idea that lower punishment cost and higher punishment effectiveness (via synergy) should increase efficiency, but this is inferred from cooperation rates, not measured payoffs.

- source: Zhu, P. C., Guo, H., Zhang, H. L., Han, Y., Wang, Z., & Chu, C. (2020). The role of punishment in the spatial public goods game. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment leads to higher levels of cooperation than pool punishment in spatial public goods games. When the threshold for switching to pool punishment is high (i.e., more peer punishment), cooperation is more prevalent. The coexistence of both punishment types is explored, and the transition between regimes is mapped. The main findings are based on the frequencies of cooperators, defectors, and punishers, not on efficiency or group payoff. The ratio of fine to cost is higher for peer punishment, which is suggested as a reason for its greater effectiveness in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that peer punishment is more effective than pool punishment in increasing the prevalence of cooperation in spatial PGGs. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model and parameterization are clearly described, so the paper can inform mechanistic expectations about how punishment type and its implementation affect cooperation, but not directly about efficiency or welfare outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that adding punishment to a public goods game model with group selection allows for the maintenance of high levels of cooperation in much larger groups than group selection alone. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation depends on the cost structure of punishment (punishment must be more costly to the punished than to the punisher, and costs to punishers must decline as defectors become rare). The model is robust to various parameter changes, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it focuses on the frequency of cooperation and punishment as the main outcomes.

- source: Traulsen, A., Hauert, C., De Silva, H., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2009). Exploration dynamics in evolutionary games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in finite-population public goods games with punishment, the rate of random exploration (mutation) crucially shapes the evolutionary outcome. For low mutation rates, defectors dominate in compulsory games, and cooperation/punishment only emerge with voluntary participation. For high mutation rates, cooperators can dominate even in compulsory games, with punishers playing a pivotal but not dominant role. The analysis is based on theoretical models and simulations, reporting the average abundance of each strategy type. The paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but the prevalence of cooperation and punishment is interpreted as beneficial for group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation in public goods games is highly sensitive to the rate of random exploration (mutation) in the population. It suggests that punishment alone does not guarantee high cooperation or efficiency unless exploration rates are high or participation is voluntary. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect: it supports the idea that punishment's effect on efficiency is contingent on the broader evolutionary or behavioral context, especially the rate of exploration/mutation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but less so for quantifying efficiency changes.

- source: Chen, X. J., Sasaki, T., & Perc, M. (2015). Evolution of public cooperation in a monitored society with implicated punishment and within-group enforcement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of implicated punishment (where all group members are fined if a defector is detected) can transform the public goods game into a coordination game, making both full cooperation and full defection stable equilibria. Increasing the monitoring probability, the implicated punishment fine, or the within-group enforcement probability all enlarge the basin of attraction for full cooperation, thus promoting higher cooperation rates. Group size has a nonlinear effect: intermediate group sizes are least conducive to cooperation, while both small and large group sizes favor cooperation. The addition of within-group enforcement (peer punishment) further relaxes the conditions for cooperation to thrive. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but higher cooperation rates in standard PGGs are typically associated with higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that implicated punishment and within-group enforcement can substantially increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when monitoring probability and punishment fines are sufficiently high. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the close relationship between cooperation rate and efficiency in standard PGGs suggests that these mechanisms would also increase efficiency. The nonlinear effect of group size is important: intermediate group sizes may be less effective for promoting cooperation than either small or large groups. For prediction tasks, the presence of implicated punishment and within-group enforcement, as well as the values of monitoring probability, punishment fine, and group size, should be considered as key moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Balliet, D., Mulder, L. B., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2011). Reward, Punishment, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, punishment_cost, reward_cost
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that both punishment and reward robustly increase cooperation in public goods and related social dilemma games. The effect of punishment is stronger when it is costly to the punisher and when participants interact repeatedly with the same group (partner matching). Decentralized punishment is more effective than centralized punishment, but this is largely explained by the correlation with costliness. Group size and cost-to-fine ratio do not significantly moderate the effect. While the main outcomes are cooperation rates, the paper discusses that punishment can reduce group efficiency in the short run (due to punishment costs) but may increase efficiency in the long run as cooperation stabilizes and punishment use declines. The effect of punishment varies by country, with some countries (e.g., Russia) showing no positive effect. The paper does not provide meta-analytic estimates of efficiency or group payoff, but references to primary studies suggest that efficiency effects are context-dependent and may be negative in the short term and positive in the long term.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods and similar social dilemma games, enabling punishment increases cooperation rates, especially when punishment is costly and the game is repeated with fixed partners. However, the paper does not provide direct meta-analytic estimates of efficiency or group payoff, which are the target outcomes for the prediction task. The discussion and references to primary studies suggest that efficiency may initially decrease when punishment is introduced (due to punishment costs), but can increase over time as cooperation rises and punishment use declines. For prediction, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be more positive in longer, repeated games with partner matching and costly punishment, but may be less positive or even negative in short or one-shot games, or when punishment is free or centralized. The lack of direct efficiency outcomes in the meta-analysis limits the precision of quantitative prediction for efficiency, but the qualitative patterns are informative for understanding moderators.

  findings: The review highlights that the inclusion of punishment and reward mechanisms in repeated public goods games and other social dilemmas enables partner control, which can promote cooperation and the development of group norms. It notes that punishment is typically costly to the punisher but more costly to the target, and that such mechanisms can shape future cooperation through reputation and indirect reciprocity. The review also discusses moderators such as group heterogeneity, intergroup competition, and cross-cultural variation in norm enforcement. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative synthesis on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.

It identifies key design features (e.g., repeated play, sanctioning options, partner selection) that are relevant to predicting the impact of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to calibrate the magnitude of punishment's effect on efficiency. Its value for prediction is mainly in identifying which game dimensions are theoretically important moderators.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schurtenberger, I. (2018). Normative foundations of human cooperation. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social norms, particularly conditional cooperation norms, are central to explaining cooperation in public goods games. Punishment opportunities increase cooperation rates, but the welfare (efficiency) effects are often limited or negative due to the costs of punishment and the occurrence of antisocial punishment. Normative constraints on punishment—such as those enabled by institutional or group-level norm formation—can reduce antisocial punishment and make punishment more effective, leading to higher cooperation with less collateral cost. The review emphasizes that the presence of punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the structure and normative context of punishment matter greatly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and integrative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff unless punishment is normatively constrained to reduce antisocial punishment and collateral costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of normative or institutional constraints, the prevalence of antisocial punishment, and the cultural context. Game design dimensions that affect the cost, targeting, and normative context of punishment are likely to be critical moderators of efficiency outcomes. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes, but it cautions against assuming that increased cooperation from punishment always translates into higher efficiency.

Punishment is frequently administered by group members or even third parties, and is motivated by non-selfish concerns for norm enforcement rather than self-interest. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency and strength, but punishment remains prevalent even when it is costly. The presence of punishment opportunities transforms the dynamics of cooperation, preventing the typical decline in contributions over time seen in standard public goods games without punishment. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment will increase efficiency, provided that efficiency is closely linked to contribution rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly, its value is primarily in establishing the behavioral mechanism and the robustness of the punishment effect on cooperation. It also highlights that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as group size and information structure, are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative estimates for these effects on efficiency.

However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report control vs. punishment-enabled efficiency rates. Compassion-based motivation, in contrast, can prevent the breakdown of cooperation in noisy environments by promoting forgiveness, but may not be effective in the long term against persistent defectors. The emotional and neural mechanisms underlying these motivations are distinct. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and thus likely efficiency), but does not allow for fine-grained prediction based on game design dimensions.
