It is directly informative for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar PGGs, especially in the Italian context. However, it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatment, so it provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency change when punishment is enabled, except as a control benchmark. The detailed reporting of game parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, feedback, no chat, etc.) makes it useful for mapping design dimensions, but not for the core prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2016). Conditional cooperation and confusion in public-goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public-goods game (4 players, 1.6 multiplier, no punishment or reward), most participants do not understand that the income-maximizing strategy is to contribute nothing, regardless of others' actions. Many participants classified as 'conditional cooperators' are actually confused, not prosocial. The same behavioral patterns are observed when playing with computers (where cooperation cannot benefit others) as with humans. There is no evidence that a subpopulation of 'comprehenders' (those who understand the game) are more cooperative with humans than with computers. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatment. However, it is highly informative about baseline (control) PGGs without punishment: it shows that observed cooperation in such games may be largely due to misunderstanding, not prosocial preferences. For prediction tasks, this suggests that control group efficiency in standard PGGs may overestimate the true baseline level of prosociality, especially if participants misunderstand the payoff structure. The paper does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency, nor does it provide evidence on moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & West, S. A. (2013). Prosocial preferences do not explain human cooperation in public-goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that (1) cooperation levels in public-goods games are not explained by prosocial preferences, as participants contribute similarly even when unaware their actions affect others; (2) providing enhanced information about others' earnings reduces cooperation; (3) even when cooperation is profitable, participants do not reach full cooperation. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. However, it does provide high-quality evidence on how information and framing affect cooperation rates in standard and variant PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does reinforce the importance of information structure and participant understanding as moderators of cooperation, which may interact with punishment in other studies.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2011). Demography and ecology drive variation in cooperation across human populations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds significant variation in cooperative behavior (measured by PGG contributions and salt-sharing) across villages of the same cultural group. Demographic factors such as age, social network size, village population size, and number of adult sisters in the village are associated with these cooperative behaviors. The results challenge the view that stable cultural norms alone explain cross-population differences in cooperation, highlighting the role of local demography and ecology. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in the games, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes were reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism was included. However, it is informative for understanding baseline variation in cooperation in one-shot PGGs without punishment, and for the influence of demographic and ecological moderators. For the downstream prediction task, this paper can inform estimates of control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar field settings, but provides no evidence on the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kurzban, R., & Houser, D. (2005). Experiments investigating cooperative types in humans: A complement to evolutionary theory and simulations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a standard public goods game (no punishment or reward), individuals fall into stable behavioral types: cooperators, reciprocators, and free-riders. The composition of these types within a group strongly predicts the group's contribution level. Groups with more cooperative types contribute more to the public good, and this relationship is robust across in-sample and out-of-sample games. Despite large behavioral differences, individual earnings are statistically indistinguishable across types. The study provides strong evidence for the existence and stability of cooperative types, and for the predictive power of group composition on cooperation, but does not address the effects of punishment or efficiency changes due to institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding baseline behavior in public goods games without punishment or reward. It provides evidence that group outcomes (contributions, and thus efficiency/payoff) are strongly determined by the distribution of behavioral types. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it manipulate or analyze any institutional design features related to punishment, reward, or efficiency interventions. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper is only useful as a control/baseline reference for games without punishment, and for understanding the role of type composition. It does not inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Brekke, K. A., Hauge, K. E., Lind, J. T., & Nyborg, K. (2011). Playing with the good guys. A public good game with endogenous group formation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when participants can self-select into groups with a prosocial commitment (charity donation), those groups attract higher contributors and sustain higher contribution rates over time compared to groups with a private bonus. The difference in contributions between group types increases over time, with blue groups showing the typical decline in contributions and red groups maintaining high levels. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding self-selection and conditional cooperation in public good games, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public good games. It is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment. However, it does provide evidence that self-selection into prosocial groups can sustain higher contributions, which may be relevant for understanding the role of group composition and endogenous group formation. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant.

- source: Smith, E. A. (2010). Communication and collective action: language and the evolution of human cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants' contributions in a one-shot, sequential PGG are strongly influenced by the observed contributions of their group members, consistent with strategic reciprocation. Individual differences in motivational traits matter: higher BAS Reward Responsiveness predicts lower contributions when others have contributed highly (i.e., more self-regarding, strategic behavior), while higher BIS-anxiety predicts a lower likelihood of making zero contributions (i.e., less free riding). The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It is useful for understanding heterogeneity in contribution behavior and the role of personality traits in cooperation, but does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design is a standard sequential PGG with no punishment or reward options, so it is only relevant as a control or baseline for contribution behavior in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Barclay, P. (2006). Reputational benefits for altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants contribute more to a public good when they expect their reputation to matter in a subsequent trust game, and that contributions are maintained best when there is competition to be the most altruistic. Trust is preferentially given to those who contributed more in the PGG. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The outcomes are primarily behavioral (contributions, trust), not efficiency or payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how reputation and competitive altruism can increase contributions, but does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. The design and findings are relevant for reputation and trust mechanisms, not for punishment or sanctioning treatments.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., Nax, H. H., & West, S. A. (2015). Payoff-based learning explains the decline in cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in repeated public goods games, participants' contribution behavior is best explained by payoff-based learning: individuals adjust their contributions in response to changes in their own payoffs, rather than out of concern for others' payoffs or by matching others' contributions. Pro-social learning is not supported and, if anything, participants appear to weight others' payoffs negatively. Conditional cooperation is only weakly supported and disappears when the consequences of cooperation are made clearer. The decline in cooperation over time is thus attributed to individuals learning to maximize their own income, not to social preferences or conditional cooperation. The paper does not study punishment or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. It is useful for understanding baseline dynamics of contribution decline in repeated public goods games without punishment, showing that such decline is best explained by payoff-based learning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper can inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency trajectory, but provides no evidence on how punishment would alter efficiency. It does, however, reinforce that in the absence of punishment, efficiency is likely to decline over time due to learning, not social preferences.

- source: Cárdenas, J. C., Janssen, M. A., Ale, M., Bastakoti, R., Bernal, A., Chalermphol, J., Gong, Y. Z., Shin, H., Shivakoti, G., Wang, Y. B., & Anderies, J. M. (2017). Fragility of the provision of local public goods to private and collective risks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the returns from the public good are risky (collective risk), participants invest less in the public good compared to when only private returns are risky or when there is no risk. Communities more integrated with the market (higher share of crops sold externally, more members with outside income) show lower cooperation, especially under collective risk. Risk aversion is associated with higher cooperation in the baseline, but not under risk treatments. The main outcome is the rate of investment in the public good; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. It does provide evidence on how risk and market integration affect cooperation rates in public good games, but not on efficiency or payoff outcomes, and not on the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly relevant.

- source: Rand, D. G., Kraft-Todd, G., & Gruber, J. (2015). The Collective Benefits of Feeling Good and Letting Go: Positive Emotion and (dis)Inhibition Interact to Predict Cooperative Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two large-sample studies using one-shot PGGs (and some continuous PDs), the presence of positive emotion in participants' narratives predicts higher contributions, while the presence of inhibition predicts lower contributions. There is a significant interaction: the positive effect of emotion is strongest when inhibition is absent. These effects are robust to controls for negative emotion and demographic variables. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze any game design dimensions related to punishment, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Its findings on psychological predictors of cooperation may be of indirect interest for understanding baseline cooperation rates in one-shot games, but are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Huang, K. K., Wang, T., Cheng, Y., & Zheng, X. P. (2015). Effect of Heterogeneous Investments on the Evolution of Cooperation in Spatial Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in cooperative investments (modeled as a uniform distribution around a mean) increases the steady-state fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. This effect is stronger with greater variance in investment. When both strategy and investment coevolve, the promotion of cooperation is weaker because investment heterogeneity is reduced. The main dependent variable is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that heterogeneity in investment can promote cooperation (as measured by the fraction of cooperators), but do not address payoff-based outcomes or the effect of punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Wang, B. K., Chen, X. J., & Wang, L. (2012). Probabilistic interconnection between interdependent networks promotes cooperation in the public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game played on two interdependent networks, the probability of interconnection between corresponding nodes in the two layers has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: an intermediate interconnection probability maximizes the fraction of cooperators in the system. The coupling effect between layers can promote cooperation in both, even if one starts with only defectors. The study does not include or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how network structure and interdependence affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based or punishment-related predictions. The absence of punishment, reward, and efficiency outcomes means it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Roca, C. P., & Helbing, D. (2011). Emergence of social cohesion in a model society of greedy, mobile individuals. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with voluntary contributions and no punishment or reward, cooperation and social cohesion can emerge if individuals have moderate levels of greediness (aspiration). Too little greediness leads to stagnation in noncooperation, while too much greediness destabilizes cooperation and social ties. The model demonstrates that these beneficial levels of greediness can be evolutionarily selected. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, agglomeration, instability), not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include or analyze punishment, and thus provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a PGG environment without punishment or reward, and focuses on the role of individual learning and mobility. While it is useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. The game design dimensions are partially covered (e.g., player count, group size, mpcr), but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot be used to predict efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Chen, M. H., Wang, L., Wang, J., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2015). Impact of individual response strategy on the spatial public goods game within mobile agents. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the order in which agents update their strategy and move (strategy update order) can significantly affect the evolution of cooperation and social instability in spatial PGGs with mobile agents. Different aspiration evaluation methods (how agents assess satisfaction based on their own and neighbors' payoffs) have only minor effects on cooperation and agglomeration, but can influence social instability. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how mobility and aspiration-based strategy updating affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. It may provide weak contextual information about the role of behavioral updating and mobility, but is not informative for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Cubitt, R. P., Drouvelis, M., Gächter, S., & Kabalin, R. (2011). Moral judgments in social dilemmas: How bad is free riding?. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that free riding is generally judged as morally blameworthy, except when both players free ride in sequence, in which case the second free rider is sometimes judged positively. Moral condemnation of free riding increases with the co-player's contribution, especially in sequential settings. Framing matters: failing to give is condemned more than withdrawing support, even when monetary outcomes are identical. The findings suggest that moral judgments in social dilemmas are sensitive to context, framing, and the actions of others, but the study does not address actual behavior, efficiency, or the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings on moral judgment may be of indirect interest for understanding motivations in social dilemmas, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Vollan, B. (2008). Socio-ecological explanations for crowding-out effects from economic field experiments in southern Africa. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment involved groups of 5 players managing a common-pool resource over 20 rounds, with a baseline (open access) phase followed by an institution phase (external penalty, external reward, or communication) chosen by group vote. Efficiency (group payoff as a percentage of the social optimum) increased from 79.6% in the baseline to 88.6% on average after introducing institutions. However, the effect of punishment (penalty) was highly contingent: when the penalty rule was imposed with low group support (low self-determination), or in high-trust Namibian communities, efficiency actually decreased (crowding-out effect), with final extraction levels as high or higher than the baseline. In contrast, in low-trust South African communities, punishment increased efficiency. Reward (external enabling intervention) did not produce crowding-out in any context and generally improved or maintained efficiency. Communication alone (no external intervention) produced stable, moderate efficiency. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed and moderated by self-determination and social trust.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is not universally positive. Instead, punishment can reduce efficiency (crowding-out) when imposed without group support or in high-trust, norm-driven communities. In low-trust settings, punishment can increase efficiency. Reward mechanisms do not crowd out cooperation and are generally safe for efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be assumed to always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the degree of self-determination (e.g., whether punishment is voted in or imposed) and the baseline level of trust in the group. Control efficiency (baseline) is a strong predictor, but the effect of enabling punishment depends on these moderators. Game design dimensions such as group voting, trust, and the nature of the intervention (controlling vs. enabling) are critical for accurate prediction.

- source: Lohse, J., & Waichman, I. (2020). The effects of contemporaneous peer punishment on cooperation with the future. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a multigenerational public goods game, voluntary cooperation is difficult to sustain across generations. When peer punishment is available within a generation, the likelihood that the group meets the contribution threshold and sustains future generations more than doubles compared to the baseline. However, the increase in group earnings from more sustained generations is offset by the costs incurred through punishment, resulting in no significant difference in average aggregate earnings (efficiency) between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The effect of punishment on individual contribution levels is small, but it is sufficient to increase the rate at which groups meet the threshold. Punishment is mainly directed at those who contribute less than their peers, especially when the group fails to reach the threshold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling peer punishment in a threshold-based, multigenerational public goods game increases the probability of group success (i.e., reaching future generations and thus group-level payoff), but the efficiency gains are offset by the costs of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of peer punishment can increase the likelihood of group success, but unless punishment is low-cost or highly effective, the net effect on efficiency may be limited or null. The design dimensions—such as group size, threshold, punishment cost and effectiveness, and the absence of communication—are all well-specified and should be used to inform predictions. The results are most relevant for settings with threshold public goods, no communication, and contemporaneous peer punishment.

- source: OSTROM, E., WALKER, J., & GARDNER, R. (1992). COVENANTS WITH AND WITHOUT A SWORD - SELF-GOVERNANCE IS POSSIBLE. *American Political Science Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a CPR game with 8 players and a quadratic production function, baseline efficiency (net yield) is low, especially in high-endowment settings. Allowing communication (covenants) alone increases efficiency substantially, with repeated communication leading to near-optimal outcomes. Introducing a sanctioning mechanism (the sword) alone increases net yield before costs, but after accounting for the costs of punishment, efficiency gains are minimal or negative. When both communication and sanctioning are allowed, especially when the group can choose the sanctioning mechanism, efficiency is maximized and defection rates are minimized. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus highly context-dependent: punishment alone is not efficient, but when combined with communication and endogenous institution-building, it is highly effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the presence of communication and the way the punishment institution is implemented. If the game design allows for communication and endogenous choice of punishment, enabling punishment can dramatically increase efficiency. If punishment is imposed without communication, efficiency gains are small or negative after accounting for punishment costs. The control efficiency (without punishment) is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect of punishment is positive only when paired with communication and institutional choice. Game design dimensions such as player count (8), number of rounds (20+), absence of chat in baseline, continuous contribution, and the specific punishment cost/tech parameters are all relevant for prediction.

- source: Kosfeld, M., & Rustagi, D. (2015). Leader Punishment and Cooperation in Groups: Experimental Field Evidence from Commons Management in Ethiopia. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This study uses a field experiment with real-world leaders of Ethiopian forest user groups, combining a third-party punishment public goods game (TPPG) with observational data on group performance in commons management. Leaders are classified by their punishment motives: non-punishers, equality-driven, equality-and-efficiency-driven, and antisocial. The main finding is that groups led by equality-and-efficiency-driven punishers achieve substantially higher efficiency (as measured by young trees per hectare) than those led by non-punishers, while groups led by antisocial punishers perform significantly worse. Equality-only punishers have no significant effect in the first assessment but a positive effect in the second. The effect sizes are large relative to the mean outcome. The results are robust to a wide range of controls and alternative specifications. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not uniform: it depends on the leader's punishment motive and implementation.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not universally positive or negative, but depends on the nature of punishment. When punishment is targeted at free riders (motivated by equality and/or efficiency), efficiency increases. When punishment is antisocial or indiscriminate, efficiency decreases. The experimental design (two-player linear PGG with third-party punishment, MPCR=0.75, punishment cost=1, punishment impact=3, no chat, continuous contributions) is close to standard PGGs, but the real-world outcome is a field measure of group welfare. The findings suggest that the presence of punishment can increase or decrease efficiency depending on the implementation and motives, and that heterogeneity in punishment type is a key moderator. For prediction, the effect of enabling punishment should be conditioned on whether punishment is likely to be prosocial (targeting defectors) or antisocial (targeting cooperators or indiscriminately).

- source: Janssen, M. A., Holahan, R., Lee, A., & Ostrom, E. (2010). Lab Experiments for the Study of Social-Ecological Systems. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic, spatially explicit CPR lab experiment with five players per group, costly punishment is used by participants but does not increase group payoffs or efficiency unless combined with communication. When punishment is available without communication, net group earnings decrease due to the cost of punishment, and resource depletion is not prevented. Communication alone leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use. When both communication and punishment are available, the positive effects of communication are not enhanced, and the use of punishment can undermine cooperative agreements once communication is removed. The study highlights that in this more complex, dynamic environment, punishment without communication is not an effective tool for increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games with real-time harvesting and five players, enabling costly punishment without communication is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no punishment, no communication) due to the direct costs of punishment. Communication is a much more effective intervention for increasing efficiency. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled (but no communication) should be lower than the control, and that the presence or absence of communication is a critical moderator. The punishment mechanism here (cost of 1 token to remove 2 tokens from another) is not sufficient to offset the cost in terms of group payoff. These results are most applicable to dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games rather than standard static PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome is directly relevant.

- source: Sigmund, K., Hauert, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2001). Reward and punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player public goods minigames with punishment or reward, evolutionary dynamics without reputation lead to asocial equilibria with low efficiency, regardless of the presence of punishment or reward options. When a small chance of reputation is introduced (players can sometimes observe others' punishment/reward behavior), the system can support a stable, high-efficiency equilibrium with cooperation and punishment. This effect is stronger and more robust for punishment than for reward. The model predicts that punishment with reputation is much more effective at promoting group efficiency than reward with reputation, and that without reputation, neither mechanism is effective in the long run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment alone does not increase efficiency in public goods games unless players have some information about others' punishment behavior (i.e., reputation). In the absence of reputation, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency; the system converges to defection and non-punishment. With even a small probability of reputation, enabling punishment can create a bistable system where high-efficiency outcomes are possible and can be risk-dominant. The effect is parameter-dependent (e.g., punishment cost, effectiveness, benefit/cost of cooperation), but the qualitative result is robust. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of reputation or information about others' actions. If the game design does not allow for reputation, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. If reputation is present, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, more so than reward.

- source: Gächter, S., Mengel, F., Tsakas, E., & Vostroknutov, A. (2017). Growth and inequality in public good provision. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dynamic public good game with endogenous endowments, contributions and group wealth increase over time even without punishment. Introducing punishment does not increase group wealth or efficiency; in some cases, it reduces them, particularly in longer games. Punishment increases contributions but destroys resources, leading to lower or unchanged group wealth. Early inequality is strongly negatively correlated with later group wealth in the punishment condition, and anti-social punishment (shirkers punishing contributors) is more prevalent in unequal groups, further reducing efficiency. Removing inequality experimentally restores the positive effect of punishment on contributions and wealth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic public good games with endogenous endowments, enabling punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to control (no punishment). The negative effect is especially likely when early inequality is high, as anti-social punishment becomes more common and destructive. For prediction tasks, if the game design includes endogenous endowments and dynamic wealth accumulation, the presence of punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency, and may decrease it, especially as the number of rounds increases. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in standard (static, exogenous endowment) PGGs, punishment increases efficiency, but in dynamic, endogenous settings, it does not.

- source: Cardenas, J. C. (2004). Norms from outside and from inside: an experimental analysis on the governance of local ecosystems. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing external regulation (punishment for over-extraction, with imperfect monitoring) or allowing face-to-face communication both lead to substantial increases in efficiency (lower extraction, higher group earnings) compared to a baseline with no intervention. The difference between high and low penalty treatments was much smaller than predicted by standard theory, with both leading to similar improvements in efficiency. A large fraction of participants complied with the social optimum extraction even when the expected cost of punishment was low, indicating that norm compliance and the salience of the rule were important. Communication (self-governance) was as effective as external punishment in increasing efficiency. The results suggest that both material and non-material incentives (norms, communication) can be effective in improving group outcomes in commons dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both punishment (external regulation with fines and monitoring) and communication (self-governance) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, even when the expected cost of punishment is low. The effect of increasing the penalty size (punishment_tech) was much smaller than predicted, suggesting diminishing returns to harsher punishment. The game design dimensions (player_count=5, num_rounds=20, no chat in baseline, chat in self-governance, continuous extraction, known payoff table, monitoring probability, and fine size) are all well specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under different punishment regimes. Control efficiency (no punishment, no communication) is lower than in the treatments, and both punishment and communication increase efficiency to similar levels.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2019). Individual solutions to shared problems create a modern tragedy of the commons. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose between solving a shared problem collectively (public good) or individually (private solution), many opt for the individual solution even when collective action would be more efficient. This leads to inefficient resource allocation and coordination failure, especially at intermediate cost-benefit ratios. When peer punishment is introduced, it increases the rate of collective solutions but also triggers mutual punishment between individualists and collectivists, which reduces overall group welfare. Punishment does not reduce free riding but instead is used in feuds between incompatible strategies, amplifying inefficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public-goods-like games where individual solutions are available, enabling peer punishment can reduce efficiency (group welfare), even if it increases collective action. The effect is driven by wasteful punishment exchanges rather than improved cooperation. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency in similar settings, especially when individual solutions are a viable alternative to collective action. The findings are most relevant when the game allows both individual and collective solutions, and punishment is peer-based, costly, and has a strong impact.

- source: ABREU, D., MILGROM, P., & PEARCE, D. (1991). INFORMATION AND TIMING IN REPEATED PARTNERSHIPS. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated partnership games with imperfect monitoring, the ability to sustain efficient cooperation through punishment is fundamentally limited by the informativeness of the monitoring technology and the timing of information. When monitoring is imperfect, reducing the interest rate (making players more patient) increases the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes, potentially allowing near-first-best payoffs. However, shortening the period of fixed action or reducing the lag with which information is reported can actually decrease efficiency, sometimes eliminating the possibility of cooperation altogether. Delaying information release can, counterintuitively, increase efficiency by reducing the cost of deterring deviations. The paper provides generalizations to n-player symmetric games and shows that these results are robust to more complex action and signal spaces.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public-goods-like games with punishment under imperfect monitoring. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the informativeness of monitoring, the timing of information, and the patience of players. In particular, enabling punishment does not guarantee high efficiency unless monitoring is sufficiently informative and the timing of information and action periods is chosen carefully. For prediction, the paper suggests that increasing patience (lower interest rate) is reliably associated with higher efficiency, but increasing the frequency of action or information updates can reduce efficiency when monitoring is imperfect. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to estimate efficiency given the relevant game design dimensions.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2018). Punishment and inspection for governing the commons in a feedback-evolving game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment and inspection mechanisms can promote cooperation and prevent overexploitation in a common-pool resource game, but their ability to improve group efficiency (resource sustainability) depends critically on the intrinsic growth rate of the resource. If the resource renews too slowly, even perfect cooperation enforced by strong punishment leads to resource depletion and low efficiency. At moderate growth rates, strong punishment and inspection can achieve both high cooperation and sustainable resource levels, but weak institutions result in mixed strategy populations and depleted resources. At high growth rates, the resource is always sustainable regardless of punishment, and punishment only affects the fraction of cooperators versus defectors. Thus, the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and can be absent or even negative if resource dynamics are unfavorable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like common-pool resource games is highly contingent on the ecological context, especially the resource's intrinsic growth rate. When predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions (such as punishment cost and effectiveness) but also the underlying dynamics of the resource. In settings where the resource cannot recover quickly, punishment may not improve efficiency even if it increases cooperation. In more favorable ecological conditions, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently effective (high detection probability and/or fine). Thus, the paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universal and must be interpreted in light of resource dynamics.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2017). Collective action and the evolution of social norm internalization. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to show that norm internalization evolves more readily and has larger effects on cooperation when peer punishment of free riders is promoted. In public-goods-like ('us-vs.-nature') games, the evolution of norm internalization and punishment increases cooperation and material payoffs (efficiency). In intergroup contest ('us-vs.-them') games, norm internalization and punishment increase cooperation but reduce material payoffs due to overproduction and rent dissipation. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in public-goods-like settings and negative in intergroup contest settings. The model also predicts that group size, punishment cost, and the strength of social norms moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the type of collective action problem. In standard public goods (us-vs.-nature) games, enabling punishment increases efficiency by reducing free riding. In intergroup contest (us-vs.-them) games, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to excessive effort and resource dissipation. The model also suggests that group size and punishment cost are important moderators. For prediction, the context of the collective action (public goods vs. contest), group size, and punishment parameters should be considered.

- source: Góis, A. R., Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2019). Reward and punishment in climate change dilemmas. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (threshold public goods games with risk of group failure), both reward and punishment policies improve group achievement (η_G) compared to no policy. Reward is particularly effective at initiating cooperation when the risk of failure is low, while punishment is more effective at sustaining high levels of cooperation, especially when the risk is high or the threshold for group success is stringent. The optimal policy is to use reward when the fraction of cooperators is low and switch to punishment when cooperation is sufficiently high. The model provides explicit formulas for the optimal switching point and shows that combining both policies synergistically yields the highest group achievement. The results are robust across a range of parameter values, including group size, population size, and policy efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (and/or reward) in threshold public goods games will increase group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to a control without such mechanisms. The model allows for direct mapping of game design dimensions such as player count, group size, number of rounds (in evolutionary time), presence and efficiency of punishment and reward, and the structure of the public goods dilemma (threshold, risk). The findings suggest that punishment is most effective at maintaining cooperation once it is established, while reward is more important for initiating cooperation. The optimal policy is a dynamic combination of both. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that adding punishment (especially with sufficient budget and efficiency) will generally increase efficiency, but the effect size and optimality depend on the risk, threshold, and whether reward is also available. The model is theoretical, so empirical calibration may be needed for quantitative predictions.

- source: Sun, W. W., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2021). Combination of institutional incentives for cooperative governance of risky commons. *Iscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk social dilemma games, institutional pure reward strategies (where executors only reward cooperators) are more effective than pure punishment or hybrid strategies in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target). This result holds for both fixed and flexible incentive schemes, and is robust to changes in risk level, mutation rate, and enhancement factor. Local sanctioning schemes are more effective than global ones. The main outcome is group achievement, not efficiency or total group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that, in collective-risk social dilemmas, institutional punishment alone is less effective than reward or hybrid schemes in promoting group achievement (a proxy for cooperation). However, because the main outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather the fraction of groups reaching a behavioral target, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction. The game structure (collective-risk, institutional incentives, evolutionary dynamics) is close but not identical to standard PGGs. The results suggest that institutional punishment may not increase efficiency as much as reward, but do not provide quantitative efficiency estimates.

- source: MCCUSKER, C., & CARNEVALE, P. J. (1995). FRAMING IN RESOURCE DILEMMAS - LOSS AVERSION AND THE MODERATING EFFECTS OF SANCTIONS. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both penalty and reward sanction systems increase cooperation in resource dilemmas, with reward sanctions producing the highest cooperation. The difference in cooperation between 'give' and 'take' frames (i.e., public goods vs. commons) disappears when sanctions are present. The effect of sanctions is robust across two experiments, and the framing of the sanction (give vs. take) affects contributions to the sanction fund but not cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect of sanctions on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that endogenous, group-funded reward and penalty sanctions increase cooperation in public-goods-like resource dilemmas, with reward sanctions being more effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The design details (e.g., player count, rounds, sanctioning mechanism) are informative for mapping to other studies, but the lack of efficiency data limits direct applicability to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Hoff, K., Kshetramade, M., & Fehr, E. (2011). Caste and Punishment: the Legacy of Caste Culture in Norm Enforcement. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that high caste individuals are much more willing to engage in costly third-party punishment of norm violations (defection in a sequential exchange game) than low caste individuals, when the victim is a member of their own specific caste. This difference is not explained by wealth, education, or political experience. The effect disappears when the victim is not a member of the punisher's own caste, indicating that in-group favouritism among high castes drives the result. The paper does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, but discusses the implications for collective action and norm enforcement capacity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish norm violations is moderated by social identity (caste status and in-group affiliation), but does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation in groups with strong in-group identity and high willingness to punish, but does not quantify efficiency outcomes. The game design is a close variant of a public goods or trust game with third-party punishment, but efficiency is not measured. Use this paper to inform moderators of punishment behavior, not direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Mussweiler, T., & Ockenfels, A. (2013). Similarity increases altruistic punishment in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that perceived similarity (induced by a cognitive priming task) increases the willingness to engage in costly punishment of uncooperative behavior in a two-person cooperation game with a punishment phase. In contrast, group identity (in-group vs. out-group) reduces altruistic punishment toward in-group members. The effect of similarity is not explained by group identity, and the two have opposite effects on punishment. The study does not directly measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the cooperation phase is single-shot, so there is no opportunity for punishment to feed back into increased cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that psychological framing (similarity focus vs. group identity) can significantly alter the frequency and intensity of costly punishment in a cooperation game with a punishment phase. However, it does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report most of the structural game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological moderators of punishment behavior, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design is limited.

- source: Tenbrunsel, A. E., & Messick, D. M. (1999). Sanctioning systems, decision frames, and cooperation. *Administrative Science Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the paper finds that introducing a weak sanctioning system (low probability of detection, small fines) in social dilemma settings reduces cooperation rates compared to no sanctions. Strong sanctioning systems (high probability, large fines) increase cooperation rates above both weak-sanction and no-sanction conditions. The effect is mediated by the decision frame: sanctions make participants more likely to view the situation as a business decision, leading to cost-benefit reasoning. When the frame is ethical, cooperation is high regardless of sanctions. The studies use two-person and multi-person social dilemma scenarios with payoff tables, but do not report group efficiency or total payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends critically on the strength of the punishment system. Weak punishment can reduce cooperation below baseline, while strong punishment can increase it. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the psychological framing induced by punishment are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported, but the results imply that adding weak punishment may decrease efficiency, while strong punishment may increase it, conditional on the induced decision frame.

- source: Mulder, L. B. (2008). The difference between punishments and rewards in fostering moral concerns in social decision making. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing the concept of punishment (without actual economic consequences) in a public-goods-like game increases cooperation and moral concerns compared to reward or control. Actual punishment (vs. reward) in a dictator-type allocation game leads to stronger moral disapproval of non-compliers and greater willingness to disadvantage them, even at a personal cost. The effect of punishment on cooperation is mediated by increased moral concerns and collective-interest motives. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment (as a concept or mechanism) increases cooperation and moral concerns more than reward in public-goods-like settings. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, which are the target for prediction. The findings suggest that, under similar game design dimensions, punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains or losses cannot be inferred from this paper. The results are most useful for predicting behavioral and attitudinal changes, not efficiency.

- source: He, N. R., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Central governance based on monitoring and reporting solves the collective-risk social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of central governance based on monitoring and reporting, with top-down punishment of defectors, can dramatically increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial collective-risk social dilemma. There are threshold effects: small increases in monitoring probability, punishment fine, or decreases in reporting cost can cause a sudden jump from low to high cooperation. The effect is stronger under harsh conditions (low synergy factor). However, the study focuses on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that top-down punishment based on monitoring and reporting can increase cooperation rates in spatial collective-risk dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar spatial PGG-like environments, enabling punishment via monitoring/reporting is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if contributions are productive, but the paper does not quantify this. The model's details (e.g., spatial structure, overlapping groups, no explicit cost of punishment to individuals) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Traulsen, A. (2012). Emergence of responsible sanctions without second order free riders, antisocial punishment or spite. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible punishment and cooperation can co-evolve and become stable in populations when interactions are non-anonymous and reputation can be built. In anonymous settings, cooperation and responsible punishment do not emerge, and antisocial punishment can persist. The model provides a threshold for the amount of information (reputation) needed for responsible punishment to be adaptive. The results are robust to errors in reputation and extend to public goods games with more than two players, though larger group sizes raise the threshold for reputation needed. The paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the emergence of cooperation and responsible punishment implies higher group welfare than in the absence of these behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games can increase cooperation and, by implication, efficiency, but only when reputation or non-anonymity is present. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by whether players can observe each other's actions and reputations. In anonymous settings, punishment does not promote cooperation or efficiency. The model also suggests that larger group sizes make it harder for punishment to be effective. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes, its value for direct quantitative prediction is limited; it is most useful for understanding the qualitative role of reputation, anonymity, and group size as moderators.

- source: Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). A bottom-up institutional approach to cooperative governance of risky commons. *Nature Climate Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that local, group-level sanctioning institutions are much more effective than global, population-level institutions at promoting successful collective action in a threshold public goods game with risk of collective loss. Local institutions increase the fraction of groups that reach the public good threshold (group achievement), especially when the perceived risk of failure is low. Global institutions are unstable and provide only marginal improvements. The effect is stronger in smaller groups. The model does not report efficiency or total payoff, but the results strongly suggest that local punishment institutions increase the likelihood of group success, which is closely related to efficiency in this context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling local, group-level punishment institutions in threshold public goods games (with risk of collective loss) will increase the rate of successful group outcomes compared to no punishment or global punishment. While the model does not report efficiency or total payoff, the 'group achievement' outcome is a close proxy for efficiency in threshold settings. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when punishment is local, groups are small, and risk perception is low. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so its value is mainly in qualitative prediction and mechanism understanding.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2013). Individual heterogeneity and costly punishment: a volunteer's dilemma. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that costly punishment, when implemented as a volunteer's dilemma, is effective in deterring defection (stealing) in groups. When only one group member needs to punish and punishment costs are heterogeneous, punishment is more efficiently coordinated (mainly by the lowest-cost punisher), and deterrence of defection is as strong as with much higher penalties in a symmetric-cost setting. The introduction of punishment reduces the stealing rate by about 50 percentage points overall, with greater reductions in conditions with high penalty or with cost heterogeneity. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment can sustain cooperation and that heterogeneity in punishment costs facilitates more effective and efficient norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong behavioral evidence that introducing punishment in a volunteer's dilemma setting (where only one punisher is needed) greatly reduces defection rates, especially when punishment costs are heterogeneous. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will likely increase efficiency (by reducing defection) in similar settings, and that cost heterogeneity among punishers can substitute for higher penalty size in deterring defection. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, the quantitative effect on efficiency must be inferred indirectly from the large reductions in defection rates. The findings are most applicable to step-level public goods games with punishment opportunities and cost heterogeneity among punishers.

- source: Guzmán, R. A., Rodríguez-Sickert, C., & Rowthorn, R. (2007). When in Rome, do as the Romans do:: the coevolution of altruistic punishment, conformist learning, and cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of both conformist learning and altruistic punishment allows for the stable maintenance of high levels of cooperation in large groups, even when migration is high and intergroup conflict is rare. Conformism preserves between-group variation and stabilizes punishment, while punishment protects against the spread of defection. The model demonstrates that cooperation can actually increase with group size when both mechanisms are present. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral frequencies (cooperation, punishment) rather than direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment (especially when combined with conformist learning) can sustain high cooperation in public-goods-like environments, particularly as group size increases. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given control efficiency and game design dimensions. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of conformism and punishment as moderators of cooperation, especially in large groups and under high migration. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2003). The nature of human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment (costly punishment of norm violators) is a robust phenomenon in laboratory experiments, including public goods games and third-party punishment games. It reviews evidence that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation in public goods games typically decays over repeated rounds, but that the opportunity for punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation. The paper also discusses the limits of altruism, noting that higher costs of punishment or contribution reduce the frequency of these behaviors. While the paper provides strong theoretical and empirical support for the importance of punishment in sustaining cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff effects from punishment interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games and similar environments can sustain higher cooperation and deter free-riding, especially in repeated or group settings. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is most useful for contextualizing the likely direction of punishment effects (i.e., more positive for cooperation and, by implication, possibly for efficiency), but it does not provide parameter estimates or direct outcome data for model training or calibration.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2015). The reputation of punishers. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.

  findings: The paper finds that the reputation consequences of punishment are context-dependent. In forced play games where punishment is possible, a punitive reputation can induce fear and stabilize cooperation, potentially supporting group efficiency. However, when partner choice is available, individuals with a nonpunishing cooperative reputation are typically preferred over punishers, unless punishment is the only available signal of cooperative intent. The efficiency of punishment (fee:fine ratio) also moderates reputational outcomes: 'efficient' punishment (fee:fine > 1) may be viewed as self-serving, while 'inefficient' punishment (fee:fine < 1) is more likely to be seen as genuinely norm-enforcing. The paper synthesizes prior empirical and theoretical work but does not provide new data or direct estimates of efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is most useful for identifying moderators and mechanisms that may affect the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public goods games and similar environments. It highlights the importance of partner choice, the efficiency of punishment, and the availability of alternative signals of cooperation. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is primarily in identifying which dimensions are likely to be important moderators (e.g., partner choice, punishment efficiency, information structure).
