For prediction tasks, the presence of forgiveness/temporary ostracism should be coded as a key moderator, and the effect of punishment on efficiency should be expected to be positive only when forgiveness is possible. The results are derived in a variable-stakes partnership game, which is adjacent to but not exactly a standard public goods game.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Indirect reciprocity provides only a narrow margin of efficiency for costly punishment. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in models of indirect reciprocity, strategies that use costly punishment (punishing defectors at a cost to oneself) are almost always less efficient than strategies that simply withhold cooperation from defectors. Costly punishment can only be the most efficient equilibrium in a very small region of parameter space, requiring fine-tuned values of benefit, cost, punishment cost, punishment impact, and reputation accuracy. In most cases, the highest group payoff (efficiency) is achieved by not using costly punishment. The results are robust to errors in reputation assignment and action execution, as long as these errors are not too frequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity settings (which are adjacent to public goods games), enabling costly punishment will almost always reduce group efficiency compared to not enabling punishment, except in a very narrow set of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this suggests that unless the game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment impact, benefit/cost ratio, reputation accuracy) are in the narrow region identified, enabling punishment should be expected to lower efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to indirect reciprocity games with binary reputation and second-order norms, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar structure.

- source: Cai, G. S., & Kock, N. (2009). An evolutionary game theoretic perspective on e-collaboration: The collaboration effort and media relativeness. *European Journal of Operational Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player e-collaboration games, introducing or increasing social punishment for defection increases the likelihood of full cooperation, which is Pareto efficient and maximizes group payoff. When punishment is low, defection or asymmetric cooperation is stable; when punishment exceeds a threshold (typically half the cost of cooperation), full cooperation becomes the unique stable Nash equilibrium. This result holds in both discrete and continuous effort models. The paper also shows that the required punishment to induce cooperation decreases as collaboration efficiency increases. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium characterizations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling or increasing punishment in a two-player, public-goods-like game will increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong relative to the cost of cooperation. The analytic results specify the threshold at which punishment changes the equilibrium from defection to cooperation. While the model is for two players and not a standard PGG, the structure is closely related, and the findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in small-group cooperation games. The results are most applicable when the punishment mechanism is salient, effective, and the cost/benefit structure is similar to the model.

- source: KANDORI, M. (1992). SOCIAL NORMS AND COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that informal sanctions (punishment by community members) can support efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, provided there is sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized labels/reputation). Under public observability, any efficient and individually rational outcome can be sustained as an equilibrium, regardless of population size or matching rule. With only private experience and no information processing, cooperation (and thus efficiency) can sometimes be sustained via a 'contagious equilibrium,' but this is fragile and breaks down in large populations. Introducing local information processing (labels/reputation) allows for robust, straightforward equilibria that sustain efficiency for any population size and matching rule. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and conditions for when these results hold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the presence of a punishment mechanism, when combined with sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized reputation/labels), enables the group to achieve efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, even with random matching and large populations. For the prediction task, this suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency to the maximum feasible level, provided the game design allows for sufficient information about past actions (e.g., public histories, visible labels, or reputation). If information is very limited (players only know their own history), punishment is less effective, and efficiency gains are limited, especially as group size increases. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes but gives clear qualitative predictions about the moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Hopfensitz, A., & Reuben, E. (2009). The Importance of Emotions for the Effectiveness of Social Punishment*. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing punishment in a sequential social dilemma (trust-like game) increases cooperation and the amount returned by second movers, compared to a baseline without punishment. However, punishment also triggers retaliation, leading to multiple rounds of costly punishment and counter-punishment. This escalation increases the cost of enforcing cooperation and can destroy resources, sometimes offsetting the gains from increased cooperation. The emotional mechanisms underlying these behaviors are explored: anger motivates punishment and retaliation, while guilt and shame can inhibit retaliation and promote pro-social adjustment. The presence of punishment opportunities does not guarantee higher group earnings or efficiency, as the costs of punishment and retaliation can outweigh the benefits of increased cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in games where punishment is possible and both parties can retaliate, the effect of punishment on efficiency is ambiguous: while punishment can increase cooperation, the associated costs (especially from retaliation) can reduce or even negate efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, this means that the presence of punishment does not always lead to higher efficiency, especially in settings where retaliation is possible and costly. The design dimensions most relevant here are the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the possibility of retaliation, and the structure of the game (sequential, two-player, not a standard PGG). Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher in the absence of costly conflict, but may be lower due to less cooperation. When punishment is enabled, efficiency may increase or decrease depending on the balance between increased cooperation and resource destruction from punishment cycles.

- source: De Dreu, C. K. W., Gross, J., Méder, Z., Giffin, M., Prochazkova, E., Krikeb, J., & Columbus, S. (2016). In-group defense, out-group aggression, and coordination failures in intergroup conflict. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases contributions to out-group aggression but does not improve the aggressor group's success rate or overall group payoffs; it leads to more wasted resources and lower individual wealth. In contrast, sequential decision-making (a coordination mechanism) increases coordination within aggressor groups, nearly doubles their success rate, and reduces wealth loss for aggressors (but increases it for defenders). The results suggest that punishment can motivate higher contributions but does not solve the coordination problem or improve efficiency, while coordination mechanisms can enhance group success and payoffs in competitive intergroup settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in intergroup contest games with costly contributions and the possibility of peer punishment, enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve group efficiency or payoffs—indeed, it can reduce them due to the direct costs of punishment. Coordination mechanisms (like sequential decision-making) are more effective at improving group success and payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment (with the cost/impact structure used here) should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, and may even decrease it. The effect of punishment is highly sensitive to the coordination context and the cost/impact structure of punishment.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Third-party punishment and social norms. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that third-party punishment is common and increases with the severity of norm violations in both dictator games (distribution norm) and prisoner's dilemma games (cooperation norm). However, the magnitude of third-party punishment is lower than that of second-party punishment. In dictator games, third-party punishment reduces but does not eliminate the profitability of unfair transfers, while second-party punishment makes unfair transfers unprofitable. In prisoner's dilemma games, third-party punishment reduces the gains from defection but does not make cooperation strictly dominant, whereas second-party punishment does. Thus, punishment mechanisms—especially when strong or when multiple punishers are present—can increase efficiency by reducing the incentive to violate norms, but the effect is stronger for second-party punishment. The paper does not directly report group efficiency as a percentage of the maximum, but provides enough data to infer that punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes by reducing the profitability of norm violations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (especially second-party, but also third-party) increases the cost of norm violations and can shift incentives toward more efficient, norm-compliant outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (with sufficient strength or multiple punishers) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to control conditions without punishment, but the effect size depends on the punishment's strength and whether it is second- or third-party. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the punishment cost/impact ratio, the number of punishers, and whether punishment is second- or third-party. The results are most directly applicable to dictator and prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to but not exact public goods games. The paper does not provide direct evidence for standard PGGs, but the mechanisms and patterns are likely to generalize to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures.

- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2003). Detrimental effects of sanctions on human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a one-shot sequential trust game, allowing the investor to impose a fine on the trustee for insufficient back-transfer leads to lower back-transfers from trustees and lower payoffs for investors, compared to both a baseline with no sanctions and to cases where the investor refrains from using the fine. The negative effect of sanctions is especially pronounced when the investor's desired back-transfer is high (i.e., when the sanction is used to enforce an unfair distribution). The paper interprets this as evidence that sanctions perceived as self-interested or unfair undermine altruistic cooperation, and thus can reduce overall efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a sequential trust game (not a standard PGG), enabling punishment (sanctions) can reduce efficiency-related outcomes (investor payoff, group surplus) compared to a no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is perceived as self-interested or unfair. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: in trust games with one-shot interactions and potential for perceived unfairness, punishment may reduce efficiency. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGGs, but highlight the importance of the perceived legitimacy and purpose of punishment as a moderator.

- source: Wang, S. X., Chen, X. J., Xiao, Z. L., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2023). Optimization of institutional incentives for cooperation in structured populations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both institutional punishment and reward can be tuned to achieve near-full cooperation in structured populations, with the optimal protocol being time-invariant and identical for both types of incentives under a given update rule. The minimal cumulative cost to the institution depends on the initial fraction of cooperators: punishment is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is high, while reward is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is low. The results are supported by analytical derivations and simulations across regular, random, small-world, and scale-free networks. The main outcome is the cumulative cost to the institution, which is a proxy for efficiency from the perspective of implementing cooperation-promoting interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the cost-effectiveness of punishment (and reward) depends on game parameters such as network structure, update rule, and initial cooperation level. While it does not report group efficiency in the standard sense, its focus on the cumulative cost to achieve high cooperation is closely related and can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in similar structured games. The results suggest that, with optimally tuned punishment, high cooperation (and thus high group efficiency) can be achieved at lower institutional cost when initial cooperation is already substantial. The findings are most directly applicable to structured Prisoner's Dilemma settings with institutional (not peer) punishment or reward, and may be less directly transferable to standard public goods games.

- source: Fehr, E., Hoff, K., & Kshetramade, M. (2008). Spite and development. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper documents widespread spiteful punishment in a one-shot trust game with third-party punishment in rural India: 61–73% of third parties punished cooperators, not just defectors. This antisocial punishment reduces the expected returns to cooperation and undermines the efficiency benefits of punishment. In a repeated stag hunt game, high-caste pairs (who are more spiteful) were much less likely to coordinate on the efficient, high-payoff equilibrium than low-caste pairs. Dictator games show that high-caste subjects are more likely to engage in payoff-reducing, spiteful choices. The findings suggest that in environments where spiteful preferences are prevalent, punishment opportunities may fail to increase, or may even decrease, group efficiency and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: when antisocial or spiteful punishment is common, punishment can reduce or fail to improve efficiency, even in games structurally similar to public goods or trust games. The presence of spiteful preferences, as measured by the frequency of punishment of cooperators and by dictator game choices, is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in settings with high antisocial punishment or spite, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and may even decrease it, especially if punishment is not tightly targeted to defectors. Game design dimensions that allow for antisocial punishment (e.g., anonymity, lack of accountability, or cultural context) should be considered as risk factors for negative efficiency effects.

- source: CLUTTONBROCK, T. H., & PARKER, G. A. (1995). PUNISHMENT IN ANIMAL SOCIETIES. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical models show that punishment can be stable and effective in animal societies, especially when dominants can punish subordinates at low cost and with high impact. Punishment can enforce cooperation, deter cheating, and maintain social order. The models identify conditions under which punishment is likely to evolve, such as strong asymmetries in power or fighting ability. Empirical examples illustrate that punishment is widespread in animal societies and can modify behavior, but the paper does not report quantitative effects on group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that punishment can increase the fitness of punishers by reducing the frequency of damaging actions by others, but do not directly address group-level efficiency as in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment can enforce cooperation and deter selfishness, which is relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in PGG-like games. However, the models are dyadic, focus on fitness rather than explicit group payoff, and do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes. The paper suggests that punishment is more likely to be effective when the cost to the punisher is low and the impact on the punished is high, and when there are clear asymmetries in power. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation and (by analogy) efficiency, but does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence for use in predictive modeling.

- source: Ågren, J. A., Davies, N. G., & Foster, K. R. (2019). Enforcement is central to the evolution of cooperation. *Nature Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enforcement mechanisms—defined as actions that reduce selfishness within cooperative alliances—are predicted by evolutionary models to evolve across a wide range of biological systems, from genomes to human societies. The models show that, in the absence of enforcement, selfishness undermines cooperation and reduces group fitness. When enforcement is possible, it evolves under many parameter regimes and leads to higher cooperation and improved collective outcomes. Empirical examples from animal societies, mutualisms, and cellular systems support the theoretical predictions. The effectiveness of enforcement depends on its cost and the structure of the system, but its presence is generally associated with higher group efficiency or fitness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment or enforcement in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency or collective payoff, relative to control conditions without enforcement. The models are not direct PGGs but are structurally analogous, and the mechanisms (punishment, policing, sanctions) map closely to punishment in PGGs. The effect is generally more positive, but the magnitude depends on the cost of enforcement and system details. The paper also highlights that enforcement is most critical when relatedness is low and that high enforcement costs can limit its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment will increase efficiency, especially when enforcement is not prohibitively costly.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., Kurzban, R., & McCullough, M. E. (2013). Do humans really punish altruistically? A closer look. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a lab setting where third-party punishment is possible but demand characteristics and audience effects are minimized, witnesses of unfairness toward others rarely engage in costly punishment. When they do, it is not motivated by moralistic anger but by envy. Victims of unfairness (those personally harmed) do punish at nontrivial rates. In hypothetical scenarios, people forecast much more punishment and anger than is observed in real behavior. The results challenge the robustness of altruistic third-party punishment in humans and suggest that previous findings may be due to experimental artefacts. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dictator/third-party punishment games where the punisher is not personally harmed, actual costly punishment is rare and mild, and not motivated by norm-enforcing anger. This suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff, since punishment is rarely used and not directed at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, and the findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is only available to third parties who are not personally affected. The results caution against assuming that punishment will improve efficiency in such designs.

- source: Martin, J. W., & Cushman, F. (2015). To Punish or to Leave: Distinct Cognitive Processes Underlie Partner Control and Partner Choice Behaviors. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dyadic allocation game with stochastic outcomes, punishment (partner control) is influenced by both the allocator's intent and the actual outcome, while partner choice (switching partners) is influenced almost exclusively by intent. The design allows for the separation of intent and outcome by having allocators choose between dice with different probabilities of fair/selfish outcomes. Responders in the punishment condition could add or subtract money (costlessly, with low probability of effect), while in the partner choice condition they could choose to stay or switch partners. The main dependent variables are the behavioral responses and their sensitivity to intent/outcome. No group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological determinants of punishment and partner choice behaviors, showing that punishment is sensitive to both intent and outcome, while partner choice is intent-driven. The game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency or total payoff is not analyzed. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may inform understanding of when punishment is likely to be used and what cues drive it.

- source: Andreoni, J., Harbaugh, W., & Vesterlund, L. (2003). The carrot or the stick: Rewards, punishments, and cooperation. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a two-person proposer-responder game, the ability to punish (reduce the proposer's payoff at a cost) increases the amount proposers share with responders, compared to a baseline with no punishment or reward. The ability to reward (increase the proposer's payoff at a cost) alone has little effect on the modal offer, though it increases variance and leads a minority to be very generous. When both punishment and reward are available, proposers make the most generous offers, and the distribution of offers first-order stochastically dominates the other treatments. Punishment and reward act as complements: punishment moves people away from selfishness, and reward encourages further cooperation. The study does not use a standard public goods game, but the findings are relevant for understanding how punishment and reward options affect cooperative behavior and, by implication, group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-person proposer-responder environment, enabling punishment increases cooperative behavior (higher offers), and that combining punishment and reward is even more effective. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings suggest that, for similar environments, adding punishment (and especially both punishment and reward) will increase efficiency relative to a baseline with neither. The effect of reward alone is weaker. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, one-shot or randomly rematched games with costly punishment and reward options. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that punishment (especially when combined with reward) will increase efficiency or group payoff, even if the effect of reward alone is limited.

- source: Gneezy, A., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2012). Conflict, sticks and carrots: war increases prosocial punishments and rewards. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of violent intergroup conflict, individuals are more willing to incur costs to punish non-cooperative group members (as measured by higher rejection rates of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game) and to reward cooperative group members (as measured by higher returns in the Trust Game when the first mover is generous). These effects are observed by comparing behavior before, during, and after the 2006 Israel-Hezbollah war among Israeli senior citizens. The findings support the hypothesis that intergroup conflict elevates sanctioning behaviors that promote within-group cooperation. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on the behavioral propensity to punish and reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that real-world intergroup conflict increases individuals' willingness to punish non-cooperators and reward cooperators in economic games structurally related to public goods dilemmas. However, because the games used (Ultimatum and Trust Games) are not public goods games and the outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results suggest that contextual factors (such as intergroup conflict) can increase sanctioning behaviors, which, in public goods games, are often associated with higher cooperation and sometimes higher efficiency, but this link is not directly tested here. The study does not manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task (e.g., MPCR, player count, punishment cost/tech, etc.), so its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes is limited.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Götte, L., Gügler, R., & Fehr, E. (2012). The mentalizing network orchestrates the impact of parochial altruism on social norm enforcement. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is strongly modulated by group membership: outgroup members who defect are punished much more than ingroup members who commit the same norm violation. This parochial punishment pattern is supported by increased activation and connectivity in a neural network associated with punishment-related decision processes (right orbitofrontal gyrus, right lateral prefrontal cortex, right dorsal caudatus) for outgroup punishment, and increased activation/connectivity in the mentalizing network (dorsomedial prefrontal cortex, bilateral temporo-parietal junction) for lenient ingroup punishment. The study provides evidence for neural mechanisms underlying parochial altruism in norm enforcement, but does not analyze or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment option leads to parochial (group-biased) punishment behavior in a third-party punishment context, with outgroup norm violators punished more than ingroup violators. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design is a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with third-party punishment, which is adjacent to but not the same as a repeated public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment can be highly group-biased, and that the behavioral effect of punishment may depend on group context, but the paper does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Apergis-Schoute, A., Herrmann, B., Lieberman, M., Müller, U., Robbins, T. W., & Clark, L. (2013). Serotonin Modulates Striatal Responses to Fairness and Retaliation in Humans. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering serotonin via ATD increases costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the ultimatum game (second-party context), but tends to decrease costly punishment in third-party punishment games. The behavioral effects are accompanied by neural changes: ATD reduces ventral striatal responses to fairness and increases dorsal striatal responses during retaliation. The results suggest that serotonin modulates the drive for retaliation rather than fairness preferences. The games are one-shot, with no repeated interaction, and the main outcomes are behavioral and neural, not payoff-based. There is no direct analysis of efficiency or group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and neurobiological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, showing that serotonin depletion increases second-party punishment but decreases third-party punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the games are not public goods games but rather ultimatum and third-party punishment games. The findings may inform predictions about the psychological drivers of punishment in similar environments, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design features (one-shot, no communication, binary accept/reject, known costs of punishment) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Böckler, A., Tusche, A., & Singer, T. (2016). The Structure of Human Prosociality: Differentiating Altruistically Motivated, Norm Motivated, Strategically Motivated, and Self-Reported Prosocial Behavior. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial behavior is multidimensional, with four robust factors: altruistically motivated, norm motivated (including punishment and reciprocity), strategically motivated, and self-reported prosociality. Punishment behavior (in second and third person punishment games) loads on the 'norm motivated' factor, indicating its role in norm enforcement. The study replicates standard findings in economic games (e.g., people punish unfairness, give more in dictator than in punishment games, help more when reciprocated), but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The factor structure is stable across two large samples. Altruistic prosociality is associated with lower negative affect and higher cognitive skills. Gender, age, and income show differential associations with the factors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment behavior is a distinct, norm-motivated component of prosociality, separable from altruistic and strategic motivations. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design details of the punishment games (one-shot, anonymous, fixed punishment effectiveness) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task. The study is most useful for understanding the motivational structure underlying punishment and cooperation, not for quantifying efficiency effects.

- source: Falk, A., Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2005). Driving forces behind informal sanctions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a three-player one-shot PD with punishment, cooperators predominantly punish defectors, and this pattern is robust across different punishment effectiveness conditions and elicitation methods. Defectors also punish, but their punishment is more likely to be spiteful and disappears when punishment cannot increase payoff differences. The cost-effectiveness of punishment is manipulated, showing that cooperators are willing to spend more to punish defectors even when punishment is less effective. The results challenge fairness theories that focus only on group-level payoffs or inequality aversion, supporting instead a model where retaliation against unfairness is central. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and motivational aspects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on the behavioral patterns and motives of punishment in a three-player PD with punishment, including how punishment is targeted and how its cost-effectiveness affects behavior. However, it does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that punishment is frequently used by cooperators against defectors, and that the willingness to punish persists even when it is costly, which may imply potential for efficiency improvements in similar games, but this is not tested or quantified here. The design details (player count, one-shot, punishment cost/effectiveness) are well specified and can inform mechanistic expectations, but not direct efficiency predictions.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Schiller, B., Rieskamp, J., Gianotti, L. R., & Knoch, D. (2014). Diminishing parochialism in intergroup conflict by disrupting the right temporo-parietal junction. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is more severe toward outgroup norm violators than ingroup violators (parochial punishment), especially in cases where the outgroup member defects against an ingroup member. Disrupting the right TPJ with rTMS reduces this parochial punishment, making third-parties treat outgroup and ingroup violators more similarly. This effect is mediated by a reduction in the motive to retaliate and a reduction in mentalizing bias (the tendency to take the perspective of ingroup more than outgroup members). The manipulation does not affect punishment in intragroup constellations or in non-defection cases. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying parochial punishment in third-party punishment games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a Prisoner's Dilemma for norm violation and a third-party punishment stage, but does not involve repeated public goods contributions or measure group efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why punishment is more severe toward outgroup members, and how neural interventions can reduce this bias. However, the results cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no efficiency or payoff data are reported. The punishment technology (1 point spent reduces target's payoff by 3) is specified, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned) and motivational (retaliation, perspective-taking), not payoff-based.

- source: Bernhard, H., Fischbacher, U., & Fehr, E. (2006). Parochial altruism in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly parochial: punishers spend more to punish norm violations when the victim is from their own group, regardless of the violator's group. Dictators (the potential norm violators) expect more punishment and transfer more to the recipient when the recipient and the third party are from the same group. When the third party is from the dictator's group, dictators expect less punishment and transfer less. The results suggest that the threat of punishment is not impartial but is shaped by group membership, leading to greater protection for ingroup victims. The paper does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are not directly about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness and application of punishment in norm enforcement are highly sensitive to group affiliations, with punishment being much more likely and severe when the victim and punisher are from the same group. However, because the experiment is a one-shot dictator game with third-party punishment (not a repeated public goods game), and because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. It is informative about the social and psychological moderators of punishment, especially parochialism, but not about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Özdemir, Y., & Fehr, E. (2014). The Value of Vengeance and the Demand for Deterrence. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that both retributive and deterrence motives drive costly punishment: participants punish norm violators even when the punishment is hidden and cannot deter future violations, but punishment is stronger when it can be communicated. Second-party punishment (when the punisher is the direct victim) is stronger than third-party punishment (when the punisher is an observer). The findings suggest that retributive motives can lead to punishment that does not increase social welfare and may be inefficient. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be motivated by retribution alone, leading to costly punishment even when it cannot improve future cooperation or group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency or group payoff, especially when punishment is driven by retributive motives rather than deterrence. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games, using a trust game with punishment, so findings may transfer in terms of understanding motives but not in terms of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kurzban, R., DeScioli, P., & O'Brien, E. (2007). Audience effects on moralistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of an audience (even just the experimenter) increases the frequency and amount of third-party punishment of norm violators in both Trust Game and sequential Prisoner's Dilemma Game settings. Under anonymous conditions, punishment is infrequent and small; when observed, punishment is more common and larger. The effect is strongest when the audience includes other participants. The study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and does not use a public goods game, but it demonstrates that reputational concerns strongly increase costly punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers informative evidence that the salience of observation (audience effects) can substantially increase the willingness to engage in costly punishment. If a public goods game design includes features that make punishment decisions observable to others, one might expect higher rates of punishment, which could (in other contexts) affect cooperation and possibly efficiency. The lack of efficiency or payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task, but the findings are relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Goette, L., Huffman, D., Meier, S., & Sutter, M. (2012). Competition Between Organizational Groups: Its Impact on Altruistic and Antisocial Motivations. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in a neutral environment, punishment is used prosocially to enforce cooperation, especially when the victim of defection is an in-group member. When competition between groups is introduced, punishment becomes antisocial: out-group members are punished more, regardless of whether they cooperate or defect. Competition increases in-group cooperation but does not reduce out-group cooperation. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the authors argue that antisocial punishment under competition is wasteful and could reduce overall efficiency, while increased in-group cooperation could enhance it. The net effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of intergroup competition can fundamentally change the function of punishment in social dilemma games: from norm enforcement to antisocial, destructive punishment. While the design is not a standard repeated public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting when punishment will be efficiency-enhancing versus efficiency-reducing. Specifically, in environments with salient group competition, enabling punishment may lead to resource-destroying antisocial punishment, undermining the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data means the findings should be used as qualitative guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2016). Looking Under the Hood of Third-Party Punishment Reveals Design for Personal Benefit. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is driven by the punisher's inference that a dictator who mistreats a recipient is also likely to mistreat the punisher. When punishers have information about how they themselves are treated, this information dominates their punishment decisions, and information about third-party treatment is largely ignored. The results support a deterrence-based account of punishment motivation. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motivations for punishment, especially the conditions under which punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not address whether punishment increases or decreases group efficiency. The design is a modified dictator/third-party-punishment game, not a standard public goods game, and no efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency.

- source: Li, J., Xiao, E., Houser, D., & Montague, P. R. (2009). Neural responses to sanction threats in two-party economic exchange. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a two-player repeated trust game, the threat of monetary sanctions (punishment) leads trustees to return significantly less to investors compared to when no sanction is threatened. This effect is robust across fair and unfair investor requests. Neural data show that the presence of a sanction threat reduces activation in brain regions associated with social reward (VMPFC, LOFC, amygdala) and increases activation in regions associated with rational, utility-based decision making (parietal cortex). The findings support the 'perception shift' hypothesis: sanction threats crowd out norm-based motivations and shift behavior toward self-interested, utility-maximizing choices. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, but the reduction in reciprocity implies a likely reduction in efficiency relative to the no-sanction condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player repeated trust game, the introduction of a punishment (sanction) threat reduces cooperative behavior (reciprocity) by trustees. While the design is not a standard public goods game, the mechanism—punishment crowding out intrinsic motivation—may generalize to PGG-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment can reduce efficiency or group payoff in environments where cooperation is norm-driven and where punishment is perceived as a signal of distrust or as crowding out intrinsic motivation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or total earnings, the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the reduction in reciprocity. The findings are most relevant for two-player, repeated, sequential games with optional punishment, and less directly for standard multi-player PGGs.

- source: Cameron, L., Chaudhuri, A., Erkal, N., & Gangadharan, L. (2009). Propensities to engage in and punish corrupt behavior: Experimental evidence from Australia, India, Indonesia and Singapore. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the propensity to punish corrupt behavior varies more across cultures than the propensity to engage in corruption. Indian subjects are most likely to offer and accept bribes and least likely to punish, while Indonesian subjects are least likely to engage in corruption and most likely to punish. The manipulation of the bribe's welfare impact (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) only significantly affects behavior in Australia, where higher harm reduces punishment rates and increases bribery. In other countries, this manipulation has little effect. The main outcomes are behavioral (bribery and punishment rates/amounts), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how punishment opportunities and the cost/harm structure of norm violations affect the willingness to punish and engage in norm-violating behavior in a corruption context. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs: it is a three-player, one-shot, sequential-move game with endogenous costly punishment, but not a repeated or group-based public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment rates and their deterrent effect are highly culture-dependent and may not always translate into higher efficiency, especially when punishment is costly and the harm from norm violation is high. The manipulation of the harm from corruption (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) shows that increasing the harm can sometimes reduce punishment and increase norm violation, due to the increased cost to the victim. This is relevant for predicting when punishment will be effective in improving efficiency, but the lack of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Delton, A. W., & Krasnow, M. M. (2017). The psychology of deterrence explains why group membership matters for third-party punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly influenced by group membership: punishers assign the most punishment when an outgroup dictator treats an ingroup recipient poorly, and the least when an ingroup dictator treats an outgroup recipient poorly. This pattern is best explained by deterrence psychology, where punishers infer that outgroup dictators who mistreat ingroup members are likely to mistreat the punisher as well. The group norm maintenance theory (fairness or parochialism norms) does not fit the observed punishment patterns. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on punishment behavior and inferences about personal valuation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological mechanisms underlying third-party punishment and how group membership moderates punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most useful for understanding when and why punishment is likely to be assigned, especially in relation to group membership, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment interventions.

- source: Shinada, M., & Yamagishia, T. (2007). Punishing free riders: direct and indirect promotion of cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that third-party punishment is group-based: cooperators in the gift-giving game punish in-group cheaters more severely than out-group cheaters, while noncooperators show the opposite pattern. Most punishment is directed at the least cooperative group members. Emotional responses (anger, unfairness, guilt) are correlated with punishment of in-group cheaters but not out-group cheaters. The design uses a gift-giving game with a public-goods-like structure, but does not directly measure or report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral allocation of punishment in group-structured social exchange games, especially regarding in-group vs. out-group targeting. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The design and findings are most useful for understanding the social and motivational determinants of punishment, not its impact on group welfare or efficiency. The punishment mechanism (costly to punisher, 3x impact on target) and the absence of feedback or repeated interaction are relevant for mapping to other designs, but the lack of efficiency data limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Cosmides, L., Pedersen, E. J., & Tooby, J. (2012). What Are Punishment and Reputation for?. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that (1) subjects use third-party reputation to regulate trust only when first-party (personal) information is unavailable; (2) punishment is not more likely to be directed at partners with a reputation for norm violation; (3) subjects are more likely to cooperate with defectors whom they have punished, consistent with punishment as a bargaining tool for future cooperation; (4) when deciding to cooperate, subjects rely on the partner's most recent behavior toward themselves, not on past reputation. The results support social exchange theory over group norm maintenance theory in the context of dyadic trust games with punishment. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological function of punishment in dyadic trust games, showing that punishment is used to bargain for better treatment rather than to enforce group norms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or a multi-player setting. The findings suggest that in small-scale, dyadic games, the presence of punishment may not increase group efficiency, but rather serves to regulate individual relationships. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether the game context is dyadic or group-based, and on the psychological mechanisms engaged. The design dimensions most relevant here are the dyadic structure, the use of reputation, and the role of punishment as a bargaining tool.

- source: Henrich, J., McElreath, R., Barr, A., Ensminger, J., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., Marlowe, F. W., Tracer, D., & Ziker, J. (2006). Costly punishment across human societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that costly punishment (both second-party and third-party) is a widespread human behavior, but its prevalence and intensity vary substantially across societies. In all populations, more unequal offers are punished more frequently, but the threshold for punishment and the willingness to punish differ. There is a strong positive correlation between a population's willingness to punish and its average altruistic behavior (as measured by dictator game offers). The findings support theories of gene-culture coevolution in human cooperation. However, the paper does not use public goods games and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment is a robust and variable feature of human behavior across societies, and that it is positively associated with altruism. However, because the experiments are ultimatum and third-party punishment games (not public goods games), and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that the effect of punishment on cooperation-related behaviors is likely to be highly variable across populations, and that willingness to punish is linked to local norms of altruism. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in repeated or group-based public goods settings.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2005). Emotion expression in human punishment behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when responders in the ultimatum game are allowed to send a message to proposers expressing their feelings about the offer, the rejection rate of unfair offers (≤20% to responder) drops from 60% (no emotion expression allowed) to 32% (emotion expression allowed). This effect is statistically significant. The ability to express negative emotions through messages appears to reduce the use of costly punishment. The distribution of offers by proposers does not differ between treatments, indicating that the effect is due to responder behavior. The study does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, but the behavioral change would mechanically increase total earnings by reducing the frequency of zero-payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in environments where costly punishment is possible, introducing a non-costly channel for emotion expression can reduce the use of costly punishment. While the study is not a public goods game, it is highly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punishment and the potential for communication or emotion expression to moderate punishment behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling communication or emotion expression may reduce the negative efficiency impact of punishment by reducing its frequency. However, the findings are specific to the ultimatum game and may not transfer directly to public goods games without further evidence.

- source: Yudkin, D. A., Rothmund, T., Twardawski, M., Thalla, N., & Van Bavel, J. J. (2016). Reflexive Intergroup Bias in Third-Party Punishment. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned to a third-party role punished out-group transgressors more harshly than in-group transgressors, especially under conditions promoting reflexive judgment (fast response or cognitive load). The effect is robust across different group identity manipulations (sports teams, nationality). The punishment is costless to the punisher and only removes money from the transgressor. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare. The findings are about behavioral bias in punishment assignment, not about the impact of punishment on cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological determinants of punishment assignment (intergroup bias, reflexivity) in a third-party punishment context, but does not provide data on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on cooperation. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games but is a one-shot, three-player 'stealing and punishment' scenario with costless punishment. Thus, the findings are not directly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. The main relevance is for understanding when and how punishment is assigned, not its effect on group welfare.

- source: de Quervain, D. J. F., Fischbacher, U., Treyer, V., Schelthammer, M., Schnyder, U., Buck, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). The neural basis of altruistic punishment. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when participants are given the opportunity to punish defectors in a trust game, they frequently do so, even at a personal cost. Effective (costly) punishment, as opposed to symbolic (non-impactful) punishment, is associated with increased activation in the dorsal striatum (caudate nucleus), a brain region implicated in reward processing. The amount of money spent on punishment correlates with the level of caudate activation, suggesting that anticipated satisfaction motivates punishment. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment compared to costless punishment, but does not eliminate it. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, but does show that punishment is used and is psychologically rewarding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, individuals are willing to pay to punish defectors, and that this behavior is motivated by anticipated satisfaction. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total welfare, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a trust game with a punishment stage. The findings support the idea that costly punishment will be used when available, but do not provide direct evidence about whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency/intensity, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barr, A., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Ensminger, J., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, J., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., McElreath, R., & Tracer, D. (2008). More 'altruistic' punishment in larger societies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the level of third-party punishment (as measured by MAO) is significantly higher in larger and more complex societies. Both local group size and ethnic group size are strong predictors of the willingness to punish unfair offers in a third-party punishment game. The results suggest that third-party punishment is more prevalent in larger societies, possibly as a response to increased anonymity and collective action problems. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the relationship between group size and the prevalence of third-party punishment in dictator-like games, which are adjacent to public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that the willingness to punish norm violations increases with group size and social complexity, which may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment is more likely to be used or effective. For the prediction task, this paper is mainly informative about the social and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Wang, C. S., Sivanathan, N., Narayanan, J., Ganegoda, D. B., Bauer, M., Bodenhausen, G. V., & Murnighan, K. (2011). Retribution and emotional regulation: The effects of time delay in angry economic interactions. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across five lab experiments, the authors show that introducing a time delay before making punishment decisions (e.g., rejecting unfair offers or punishing norm violators) generally reduces costly punishment, but only if the delay is accompanied by distraction or cognitive reappraisal. If participants ruminate during the delay, punishment increases. The effect is mediated by negative emotions (anger). The experiments use one-shot or single-decision ultimatum games and third-party punishment trust games, with punishment measured as rejection rates or points spent. No direct group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, but the reduction in costly punishment implies less wasteful destruction of resources. The findings highlight the importance of emotional regulation in moderating punitive impulses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the context and psychological state of punishers (e.g., time delay, distraction, rumination) can strongly moderate the frequency and magnitude of costly punishment. While the games are not public goods games, the results suggest that interventions reducing negative emotion or promoting reappraisal/distraction could reduce inefficient punishment and thus improve group payoffs. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, and the games are not repeated or multi-player PGGs, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence for prediction tasks.

- source: Shinada, M., Yamagishi, T., & Ohmura, Y. (2004). False friends are worse than bitter enemies: Altruistic punishment of in-group members. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The bidirectional supervision mechanism, where players can check and avoid corrupt umpires, is effective at suppressing both corruption and defection in a spatial donation game with third-party punishment. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the punishment amount (must be within a certain range), the bribe amount (must not be too high), and the ratio of tax to checking cost (higher tax or lower checking cost is better). The benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation has little effect within reasonable ranges. The main outcomes are the fractions of different strategies (cooperation, defection, corruption, fairness) in the population. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but argues that the mechanism is most beneficial to collective interests when parameters are tuned appropriately.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the design of punishment, bribe, tax, and checking cost parameters can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation and corruption in a donation game with third-party punishment. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as qualitative or mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction of efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is only effective at suppressing defection and corruption if its severity is within a certain range and if corruption incentives (bribes) are not too high. The presence of a mechanism for players to avoid corrupt punishers is critical. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs with third-party punishment and corruption, but less directly transferable to standard public goods games or to quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Espín, A. M., Exadaktylos, F., & Herrmann, B. (2014). Fair and unfair punishers coexist in the Ultimatum Game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both prosocial and antisocial individuals engage in costly punishment in the Ultimatum Game, as measured by rejection of unequal offers. Prosocial punishers are those who also behave fairly in the Dictator Game, while antisocial punishers are unfair as dictators but still reject unequal offers in the Ultimatum Game. The coexistence of these types is robust across two large-scale experiments (one with a representative city sample, one with university students). The paper highlights that antisocial punishment may undermine the efficiency and stability of social systems, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it focuses on the motivations behind punishment in the Ultimatum Game. However, it offers important contextual evidence that not all punishment is prosocial or norm-enforcing; antisocial punishment can exist and may have negative implications for efficiency in broader social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency, especially if antisocial motives are prevalent. The findings are most relevant for understanding heterogeneity in punishment motives, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Promoting cooperation by punishing minority. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a multilayer network with inter-layer feedback and reinforcement learning, cooperation is promoted by lower punishment costs and higher punishment severity. The frequency of cooperation is sensitive to these parameters, with cooperation collapsing when punishment is too costly or too weak. The self-adapting Q-learning mechanism enables agents to learn strategies that support higher cooperation rates, especially when learning rates and discount factors are tuned appropriately. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost and severity affect cooperation rates in a spatially structured, multilayer environment with reinforcement learning. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation under certain cost/severity regimes, but excessive punishment cost can undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results are only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify group payoff or efficiency. The model is also not a standard public goods game, but rather a spatial prisoner's dilemma with punishment administered by a management layer.

- source: Wang, Z., Xia, C. Y., Meloni, S., Zhou, C. S., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Impact of Social Punishment on Cooperative Behavior in Complex Networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
