The meta-analyses focus on the effect of artificial surveillance cues on generosity, not on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency. While one included study uses a public goods game, the outcome is individual generosity, not group efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Ember, C. R., Skoggard, I., Ringen, E. J., & Farrer, M. (2018). Our better nature: Does resource stress predict beyond-household sharing?. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that beyond-household food and labor sharing is nearly universal in nonindustrial societies. Societies experiencing higher resource stress (e.g., famine, natural hazards, chronic scarcity) are more likely to have frequent and extensive sharing customs, especially seasonal sharing. However, increased resource stress does not predict broader social or geographic scope of sharing (i.e., with non-relatives or beyond the community); in some cases, sharing with non-relatives is less common under high resource stress. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve experimental manipulation or public goods game paradigms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve experimental games, punishment, or payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides contextual evidence that cooperation (in the form of sharing) is more frequent in environments with higher resource stress, suggesting that environmental risk can increase cooperative customs. This may be indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in environments with or without punishment, but does not inform the effect of punishment or game design dimensions on efficiency.

- source: Grujic, J., Eke, B., Cabrales, A., Cuesta, J. A., & Sánchez, A. (2012). Three is a crowd in iterated prisoner's dilemmas: experimental evidence on reciprocal behavior. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in iterated multiplayer Prisoner's Dilemma games, cooperation is sustained and even increases in two-player groups, but decays rapidly and remains low in groups of three or more. The main behavioral mechanism is 'moody conditional cooperation,' where players' likelihood to cooperate depends on both their own previous action and the number of cooperators in the previous round. There is substantial heterogeneity in initial generosity but less in reciprocity. The presence of more than two players leads to a sharp drop in sustained cooperation, suggesting that reciprocity alone is insufficient to maintain cooperation in larger groups without additional mechanisms such as punishment. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, increasing group size from 2 to 3 or more sharply reduces sustained cooperation in iterated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, it suggests that, without punishment, efficiency (if measured) would be high in two-player games and low in groups of three or more. However, because the study does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, it cannot directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of group size as a moderator of cooperation and suggests that additional mechanisms (like punishment) may be necessary to sustain cooperation and efficiency in larger groups.

- source: Swakman, V., Molleman, L., Ule, A., & Egas, M. (2016). Reputation-based cooperation: empirical evidence for behavioral strategies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that people condition their helping decisions on both first-order (recipient's past helping) and second-order (context of recipient's past helping/defection) information. Donors are especially interested in 2nd-order information about defection, and tend to reward 'justified defection' (denying help to defectors). There is strong individual heterogeneity: some use only first-order information, some use both, and a minority are unconditional cooperators or defectors. The availability of 2nd-order information (free or costly) does not significantly increase overall helping rates compared to the control, though helping rates are numerically higher when 2nd-order information is available. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The game is an iterated indirect helping game with binary helping decisions, and the main outcomes are behavioral (helping rates, information use, strategy classification). While the findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms of reputation-based cooperation, they do not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The only indirect relevance is that richer information about others' behavior can (non-significantly) increase helping rates, but this is not about efficiency or payoff, nor about punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., Smyth, C., Mylona, K., & Niblo, G. A. (2014). Benevolent Characteristics Promote Cooperative Behaviour among Humans. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) benevolence—defined as unilaterally increasing another's payoff beyond one's own—exists in the population, (2) individuals who are more benevolent are more likely to cooperate in a subsequent one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, and (3) priming participants to think benevolently increases their cooperation rate compared to priming malevolence. The study uses custom BT games and standard two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, but does not include public goods games, punishment, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about benevolence and cooperation may be contextually relevant for understanding individual factors that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Romano, A., Sutter, M., Liu, J. H., Yamagishi, T., & Balliet, D. (2021). National parochialism is ubiquitous across 42 nations around the world. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people across 42 nations cooperate more with ingroup (same-nation) partners than with outgroup or unidentified partners in a one-shot, continuous prisoner's dilemma. This national parochialism is highly consistent across nations and is not explained by cultural distance, institutional quality, or ecological factors. Observability (public vs private) increases overall cooperation but does not moderate parochialism. There is substantial cross-national variation in baseline cooperation rates (impersonal cooperation), which is associated with cultural and ecological factors. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design (continuous PD, no punishment, no repeated interaction) is adjacent to but not directly informative for the downstream prediction task. The only potentially relevant dimension is the effect of observability (public vs private) on cooperation, but this is not tied to efficiency or payoff, and there is no punishment treatment.

- source: Gächter, S., Gerhards, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2017). The importance of peers for compliance with norms of fair sharing. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of peers strongly influences what participants perceive as socially appropriate sharing, but this does not translate into corresponding changes in actual sharing behavior. There is substantial heterogeneity in both norm perceptions and willingness to comply with norms. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how peer information shapes norm perceptions and sharing behavior in dictator-like settings, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Haley, K. J., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2005). Nobody's watching? Subtle cues affect generosity in an anonymous economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that subtle visual cues of being watched (eyespots) significantly increase generosity in a one-shot dictator game, as measured by both the mean amount allocated and the proportion of dictators who give any money. The effect of reducing auditory cues (earmuffs) is small and not statistically significant. The results suggest that even in anonymous, noniterated economic games, intuitive or affective cues of observability can increase prosocial behavior. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Its findings about subtle cues of observability increasing generosity in dictator games may be contextually interesting for understanding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Cesarini, D., Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johannesson, M., Lichtenstein, P., & Wallace, B. (2008). Heritability of cooperative behavior in the trust game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in trust and trustworthiness in the trust game are partially explained by genetic variation, with heritability estimates ranging from 10-20% for trust and 17-18% for trustworthiness. The majority of behavioral variance is due to unshared environmental factors. There is little evidence that common environment (e.g., family upbringing) explains much of the variance. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game studied (trust game) is only adjacent to public goods games. The findings are only relevant for understanding the sources of individual variation in cooperative behavior, not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Epstein, Z. G. (2014). Risking Your Life without a Second Thought: Intuitive Decision-Making and Extreme Altruism. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who engage in extreme altruism overwhelmingly describe their decision-making as intuitive, fast, and automatic, rather than deliberative or reasoned. This is supported both by human ratings of interview statements and by linguistic analysis of inhibitory language. The results are robust even in cases where the altruist had time to deliberate. The study does not involve any economic games, public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions of interest (e.g., player count, punishment, MPCR, etc.), nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about intuitive decision-making in real-world extreme altruism may be of theoretical interest for understanding the psychology of cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Buckholtz, J. W., Martin, J. W., Treadway, M. T., Jan, K., Zald, D. H., Jones, O., & Marois, R. (2015). From Blame to Punishment: Disrupting Prefrontal Cortex Activity Reveals Norm Enforcement Mechanisms. *Neuron*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC) plays a selective, causal role in punishment decision-making for norm violations. Disrupting DLPFC function via rTMS reduces the amount of punishment assigned to culpable agents, especially for low- to moderate-harm crimes, but does not affect blameworthiness ratings. fMRI results show that DLPFC activity is higher during punishment decisions than during blameworthiness judgments. The study supports an integration-and-selection model in which DLPFC integrates information about harm and culpability to determine punishment. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any economic game with payoffs, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not use such games or report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding the cognitive and neural mechanisms of punishment assignment in norm enforcement, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency or group payoff in economic games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context about punishment, but not for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

- source: Falk, A., Becker, A., Dohmen, T., Enke, B., Huffman, D., & Sunde, U. (2018). GLOBAL EVIDENCE ON ECONOMIC PREFERENCES. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds substantial heterogeneity in economic preferences across and within countries. Preferences such as patience, risk tolerance, altruism, reciprocity, and trust are systematically related to individual characteristics (age, gender, cognitive ability) and to country-level geographic and cultural variables. Patience is strongly correlated with economic development (GDP per capita), while negative reciprocity is correlated with the frequency of armed conflicts. Social preferences (altruism, positive reciprocity, trust) are associated with prosocial behaviors and charitable activities. However, the study does not involve public goods games, group efficiency, or experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it measures negative reciprocity (willingness to punish unfairness) as a preference and relates it to macro-level outcomes (e.g., conflict), it does not analyze the effect of punishment opportunities on group payoff, efficiency, or cooperation in controlled game settings. Therefore, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very indirect context on the prevalence of punishment motives across societies.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2009). Experimental Games and Games of Life among the Ju/'hoan Bushmen. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The Ju/'hoan Bushmen made low offers and rarely punished in anonymous dictator and ultimatum games, behaving more selfishly than in their daily lives, where sharing and social sanctions are common. The anonymity and lack of social context in the experimental games removed the cultural institutions that normally support generosity and punishment. In daily life, sharing is widespread and norm violations are sanctioned, usually through verbal criticism and exclusion rather than costly punishment. The study highlights the importance of cultural context and institutions for the expression of other-regarding behavior and social sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a public goods game or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the importance of social context and cultural institutions for cooperation and punishment, but do not inform quantitative predictions about punishment's effect on efficiency in experimental economic games with or without punishment. The study suggests that the presence or absence of social context and identification may strongly moderate the willingness to punish or cooperate, but provides no direct evidence for the target prediction task.

- source: Mazar, N., Amir, O., & Ariely, D. (2008). The Dishonesty of Honest People: A Theory of Self-Concept Maintenance. *Journal of Marketing Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across six lab experiments, the authors find that (1) people cheat when given the opportunity, but only by a limited amount; (2) reminders of moral standards (e.g., Ten Commandments, honor code) eliminate or reduce cheating; (3) increasing categorization malleability (e.g., using tokens instead of money) increases cheating; (4) cheating is not sensitive to the probability of being caught or the magnitude of external rewards within the tested range; (5) cheating does not affect self-reported self-concept as honest/moral; and (6) beliefs about others' cheating do not affect individual cheating. The results support a psychological model of dishonesty based on self-concept maintenance, not external punishment or cost-benefit analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or public goods outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not manipulate or analyze punishment or sanctions as a treatment, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding psychological mechanisms of norm compliance and dishonesty, and may be indirectly informative for the design of interventions (e.g., moral reminders) to reduce cheating, but do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Gould, R. V. (2000). Revenge as sanction and solidarity display: An analysis of vendettas in nineteenth-century Corsica. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that only about 10-15% of violent attacks were followed by revenge, and that such retaliation was most likely when the original attack involved multiple assailants (collaboration) or targeted nondisputants (kin uninvolved in the dispute). Revenge was not indiscriminately directed at all kin of the offender, but rather at those who had demonstrated solidarity with the original offender, either by collaborating in the attack or by aiding the offender afterward. The structure of revenge mirrored the kin relationships involved in the original offense, suggesting a strategic, solidarity-signaling function. The findings challenge the idea of automatic collective responsibility and instead support a model of 'contingent collectivism,' where group-level retaliation is conditional on group-level aggression.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about the social logic and structure of revenge in a real-world, stateless society context, not about experimental games or efficiency. However, it may offer indirect, contextual insight into the conditions under which group-level sanctions (punishment) are likely to be deployed: namely, when group-level (collaborative) violations occur. For the prediction task, this suggests that the presence of group-level aggression or collaboration may be a moderator of the likelihood or intensity of punishment, but the paper does not address efficiency or payoff consequences.

- source: Kasper, C., & Mulder, M. B. (2015). Who Helps and Why? Cooperative Networks in Mpimbwe. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that in a rural Tanzanian village, helping behavior is generally low and is best predicted by reciprocity (contingency), with kinship and affinal relationships also playing roles. Wealthier individuals are less likely to participate in reciprocal helping relationships and receive less help. Geographic proximity and age also influence helping patterns. The study discusses the erosion of traditional cooperation networks in the context of increasing market integration and inequality. Witchcraft accusations are mentioned as a form of social sanction, but there is no formal punishment system or experimental manipulation of sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or any formal game, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. The study is observational, not experimental, and focuses on naturalistic helping behavior, not on the effects of introducing or varying punishment mechanisms. Witchcraft accusations are discussed as a form of social pressure, but not in a way that is structurally or functionally equivalent to PGG punishment treatments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some adjacent context about the role of social sanctions in real-world cooperation, but not in a way that is mappable to the target prediction problem.

- source: Exadaktylos, F., Espín, A. M., & Brañas-Garza, P. (2013). Experimental subjects are not different. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both student status and self-selection (volunteering) can have small effects on social preference behaviors, but these effects are generally minor and not economically significant. Self-selected students (the typical lab subject pool) behave very similarly to the broader population in Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. The only significant differences are small and mainly concern strategic behavior and trust/reciprocity among non-students. The results support the external validity of using self-selected students for social preference experiments, but do not address public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any public goods game, punishment, or sanctioning treatment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are only relevant to the representativeness of subject pools in social preference experiments using Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Sutter, M., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Lergetporer, P. (2018). Language group differences in time preferences: Evidence from primary school children in a bilingual city. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that German-speaking children are significantly more patient (more likely to delay gratification) than Italian-speaking children, even after controlling for age, IQ, risk attitudes, and family background. This difference is robust across different elicitation methods and is also reflected in self-reported saving behavior. Children from bilingual households show intermediate levels of patience. The study does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not study group cooperation, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on time preferences and cultural/language group differences in patience are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in public goods games.
